## Global Data Watch

Support for broader growth in May global PMI and US jobs report

• Fed ease not expected until November

Election surprises generated greatest uncertainty for South Africa

Next Week: Fed trims 2024 easing; firm US core CPI; BoJ starts QT

## Fighting fear, questioning hope

Our 2024 narrative can be characterized as a push-back against growth fears and disinflation optimism. While anticipating a US-led downshift in global GDP growth, we have downplayed the risk of a material near-term threat to the expansion. Indeed, the global expansion is expected to move onto firmer ground in 1H24 as growth broadens across regions and sectors. At the same time, we are skeptical of the widespread optimism for an immaculate disinflation that opens the door for significant central bank easing. We expect the global and US economies to end the year close to where they began – with core CPI rising at a roughly 3%ar.

In a week that saw the ECB and Bank of Canada start easing, incoming data points to a limited window for central bank rate cuts. This week<sup>’</sup>s most decisive news came from our all-industry global output PMI, which moved up for the seventh straight month in May. Although we discount the survey<sup>’</sup>s signal that global growth is running at a robust 3.4%ar pace, we put considerable weight on the sectoral and regional details of the survey that point to a global expansion less reliant on a US demand engine (Figure 1). In addition, there are encouraging signs that the expansion in the global manufacturing sector is broadening beyond the tech exporting Asian economies.

Tempering the PMI signal of accelerating GDP gains are softer activity readings in the US and China. The US slowdown to a projected 1.7%ar gain in 1H24 is coming largely from two anticipated developments—a fading fiscal stimulus and building net trade drag. Through this slowing, support for sustained US expansion

stands on the back of a business sector translating profit gains and still supportive financial conditions into hiring and spending. Today<sup>’</sup>s US employment report is encouraging as it is shows sustained solid labor income gains and a broadening base of US service sector job gains (Figure 2). A complementary signal is coming from indicators that US capital equipment spending is now lifting.

Corporate profits have surprised to the upside elsewhere over the past year, suggesting that the foundation of a healthy corporate sector extends beyond the US. A tumble in global business equipment spending (capex) alongside a stall in capex nowcasters late last year raised some concern that animal spirits may be pulled back. The recovery signaled by highfrequency data boosting our nowcasters over the first five months of this year bolstered our confidence that spending would rebound. This view has now been validated as business capex reaccelerated last quarter (Figure 3). Our capex nowcaster continued to accelerate through May—boosted notably by a welcome bounce in the capital goods PMI.

Figure 3: Global capex, actual and nowcaster
![](images/66fa27ca8cc6a3692a003fa60059288e56dec3201db2c0df42782d5f84f13612.jpg)

There has been less incoming inflation news, and this week<sup>’</sup>s US labor market report, showing firmer than expected wage gains and a rising unemployment rate, sends mixed signals. However, a surprising strong Euro area May core HICP report is expected to be followed by a firm 0.33% m/m US core CPI gain next week. With PMI output prices edging higher and the global expansion standing on solid ground, it is unlikely that a significant downshift in core inflation is in the offing.The prospect that sticky inflation slows central bank easing is not lost on markets, which has repriced the path ahead for global policy rates in response to this week<sup>’</sup>s news.

## Trust but verify

The ECB and Bank of Canada easing this week came with guidance from both central banks that the path ahead will likely see rates continue to move lower. For Canada and Sweden, further easing looks likely as inflation has moved close to central bank targets. However, for the ECB, Fed, and Bank of England inflation remains elevated and recent readings have surprised to the upside. With our expectations for solid growth to be sustained in these economies through the rest of this year, a patient shallow path of easing is the most likely outcome.

This point was underscored in this week<sup>’</sup>s ECB staff projections. Revisions were firmer on near-term growth and employment and lower on unemployment and productivity. All of this contributed to pushing the core inflation forecast higher. Although President Lagarde noted that there is a “strong likelihood<sup>”</sup> that the ECB has entered the “dialing back<sup>”</sup> phase of rates from a perceived restrictive stance, the burden is now falling on the data to provide reassurance that the disinflation process in on track to deliver an easing at the next forecast update in September.

The Fed will almost certainly remain on hold next week and move its projections in offsetting directions. 2024 GDP growth is likely to be revised down, to around 1.5%, and core PCE increases revised up, to 2.8%. With the Fed likely to align with our views on growth resilience, inflation persistence will likely dominate the debate. Most likely, this year<sup>’</sup>s projection of three cuts will be trimmed. The Committee will likely be finely divided between one and two cuts this year, and we believe a dovish leaning Chair likely tips the balance toward two. Our US team shifted its view this week to anticipate only one ease this year coming in November.

## EM election surprises raise policy risks

Election results in three major EM economies this week brought material surprises. Two of them (South Africa and India) resulted in significant erosion of support for ruling parties, whereas Mexico<sup>’</sup>s Morena won an unexpectedly large landslide victory. Post-election uncertainty appears highest in South Africa as the ANC faces the complex task of forming a coalition of ideologically-disparate opposition parties. This comes against a challenging economic backdrop of weak growth and poor post-pandemic labor market performance (Figure 4). In all three cases, uncertainty over economic policies looks to have risen, each with its unique set of challenges:

South Africa<sup>’</sup>s ANC this week affirmed its commitment to seek a government of national unity (GNU). But forming such a union may not be workable. Indeed, the EFF party ruled out participation in the GNU, objecting to the potential participation of the DA party. While its stance may well soften, a minority government with an outside agreement with the DA may be a more likely outcome. Negotiations over the next week will be crucial, and most options are still on the table.

India<sup>’</sup>s government formation should be somewhat easier as the ruling BJP can rely on its NDA partners to form a coalition. However, it also raises the risk of more fiscal slippage. Thus far, the policy focus has been squarely on shoring up the supply side of the economy, and it remains to be seen if the next administration pivots to boosting demand. The upcoming FY25 Budget presentation next month will offer an important first glimpse into the new government<sup>’</sup>s thinking.

In Mexico, Morena<sup>’</sup>s clear sweep makes it likely it will have a two-thirds supermajority in both houses of Congress. This would allow it to change the constitution and enact some changes that AMLO was unable to push through, including to the judiciary. The reappointment of the orthodox Finance Minister and Sheinbaum<sup>’</sup>s stated support for the central bank<sup>’</sup>s autonomy are encouraging. Nonetheless, it will be important to monitor changes within the Supreme Court and the Banxico board later this year.

## BoJ to start QT process

As the rest of the world eyes dialing back tight monetary policies, Japan is still seeking to remove accommodation. The BoJ likely will take another step at next week<sup>’</sup>s policy meeting, announcing a start of QT. Since the NIRP removal in March, the BoJ has kept the enormous size of its balance sheet unchanged by broadly matching monthly JGB purchases with the scheduled redemptions. However, as more rate hikes are on the horizon (we expect a July hike), the BoJ intends to restore functionality in JGB market pricing. While the initial pace of QT likely will be gradual, we think the key focus on the decision is whether the BoJ will reduce its discretion of the amount of JGB purchases in the QT process.

## China domestic and foreign uncertainties

The projected sharp deceleration in Chinese activity this quarter can been seen in recent activity data. Spending over the recent holiday showed stronger tourism but lower spending per capita. The two sets of PMI readings in May moved in opposite directions: NBS reported a decline in both manufacturing and non-manufacturing PMI, while Caixin reported increases in both PMI readings with manufacturing PMI hitting a two-year high. May trade reports showed relatively stable performance, with exports declining 0.9%m/m sa and

imports ticking up 0.2%m/m sa. However, divergences in external demand raise uncertainties. Exports to EM countries in May significantly outperformed exports to the US and EU, both of which contracted. The diversification of China<sup>’</sup>s exports away from the major DMs could prove important in helping to sustain growth given the intensifying trade war. After the latest tariff increase on US\$18bn yuan Chinese goods by the Biden administration, next week the EU will discuss a possible tariff increase on China<sup>’</sup>s EV imports, and Trump has threatened to hike tariffs on Chinese goods to 60% if he is re-elected.

## Asian tech lifts, but with offsetting drags

Asia<sup>’</sup>s tech bellwethers reported strong gains in their May manufacturing PMIs with Korea and Taiwan reaching twoyear highs. We expect Korean IP to expand a solid 5%ar this quarter. Together with strong April IP and May customs exports data, we expect the tech sector should take a lead this year on upbeat AI-related demand. We also look for Taiwan IP to sustain solid upward momentum as tech demand strengthens. But tech strength is being countered by more sluggish non-manufacturing activity, held back by softer domestic demand. Moreover, idiosyncratic factors are threatening the auto sector. A safety testing scandal in Japan is broadening across the auto sector. Temporary halts will weigh on what had been a nascent recovery in the goods producing sector. The risk is that the drag disrupts regional or even global supply chains.

## CBR and CBRT on hold for longer

Sticky inflation is causing central banks in Russia and Turkiye to postpone rate cuts and even raise the specter of renewed hikes. Russia<sup>’</sup>s CBR stayed on hold this week but signaled readiness to hike in July amid a still-overheated economy. We anticipate a softening in the data flow will avert further policy tightening but see rates remaining higher for longer: we pushed back the first cut to later in the year and see the policy rate at 15% at end-2024 (150bp higher than previously). In Turkiye, May inflation surprised on the upside with core CPI momentum still elevated at 3.2% m/m due to robust service price gains. We maintain that inflation will ease to 43.5% oya by year-end but see risks skewed to the upside. We still expect the CBRT to cut its policy rate from 50% to 45% in the last two months of the year, but risks are for no cuts this year.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 2Q24 global real GDP growth held steady this week at 2.3%ar (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster ticked up 0.1%-pt to 2.3%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth (Table 2).

One-quarter of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in just under half of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests a pickup in capex growth from 2.4%ar in 1Q24 to 3.1%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

The May global all-industry PMI rose 1.3-pt as both Western Europe and the manufacturing sector PMIs continued their recoveries. The global business confidence recovery resumed last month, while global consumer confidence weakened for the second straight month in May.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bond yields were modestly lower this week amid notable intra-week volatility. In the US, an initial rally amid softer claims and JOLTS data was followed by a significant reversal after the labor market report. Our economists now expect it to take longer to show a “broader<sup>”</sup> easing in labor markets and see a first cut in November followed by quarterly cuts in 2025. With yields close to levels where we added duration last week before taking profit, we would look to reenter if entry points are sufficiently attractive. We keep 5s/30s steepeners as a medium-term bullish proxy, acknowledging somewhat reduced conviction given a later start to the easing cycle.

In the Euro area, the ECB delivered a cut with a hawkish set of forecast revisions and struck a cautious tone over elevated domestic price pressures and wage growth (ECB cuts rates, Jun 6). We have a medium-term bullish bias and see nearterm range trading. We reenter tactical longs in 1Yx1Y €STR after taking profit post-ECB and hold 10Y/15Y Germany and 2Yx2Y/10Yx10Y EUR swaps steepeners. In the UK, surveys suggested a drop in forward-looking wage expectations, but the next few inflation prints are key to an August BoE cut. We keep fronts/reds SONIA curve flatteners as a positive carry bullish proxy.

In EM, growth is set to slow in 2H24 after upside surprises in 1H. At the same time, a more differentiated progress on disinflation has led to a slowing in EM monetary easing, with further cuts heavily dependent on the Fed. We stay neutral on local market duration, with OWs in Latam (Brazil, Colombia, and Uruguay) and EMEA EM (Poland and Türkiye) offset by a short duration overlay and an UW in EM Asia via Thailand (EMOS, Jun 6).

## Credit

US HG spreads finally break out of their tight range, to the high side, with lower yields. This yield rally, particularly strong since late April, has been partly driven by weaker economic data indicating a slowdown in growth. We do not believe supply is set to pick up near term. On the demand side, spreads have widened 5bps to 104bp this week, suggesting a pullback by some investors due to less attractive yields. Additionally, a decline in US yields has made it less appealing for overseas investors, particularly with the ECB rate cut. However, overnight buying in Asia remains robust. Retail demand for HG bond mutual funds and ETFs has been weak recently, but strong recent returns might boost this segment. Overall, HG bond yields remain attractive versus the longer term average even after this move lower (CMOS, Jun 7).

Primary activity in May was a record for leveraged loans and a high since Sep<sup>’</sup>21 for HY bonds. US HY bonds provided their strongest gains of 2024 in May amid strong earnings, the largest retail inflows since November, an active primary market, and a cooperative macro narrative that resides between Goldilocks and a Soft Landing. US HY bond yields decreased 13bp and spreads increased 6bp in May to 8.14% and 349bp. Leveraged loans posted a seventh consecutive monthly gain in May amid a solid earnings season, a historic repricing wave, steady retail inflows, and the second most active month of CLO origination on record (HY Bond and LL Market Monitor, Jun 3). While there were no actual defaults in May (Ch.11, missed int./prin.) for the first time since December 2022, distressed exchange activity was the third largest monthly total on record. Notably, the gap between leveraged loan and HY par-weighted default rates is at a high since Dec-14. We reduce our 2024 forecast for HY bond default rates to 2.00% and raise our leveraged loan default rates forecast to 3.75% (Default Monitor, Jun 3).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns
![](images/f0a3764232906d2e82de941e6bfea5bd21eb08b5d64faceeb90f66d7ef7af7eb.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

Euro credit markets again showed little response to recent catalysts, with HG cash spreads trading in a 0.8bp range for the whole week. We continue to think that investors have become largely desensitized to headline noise after four years of history-defining events, which will keep volatility low provided that the economy remains broadly healthy and the narrative does not shift all the way toward renewed rate hikes by central banks. The stability in spreads came despite another €19bn pricing in primary markets. With this, YTD supply is at €348bn, nearly €7bn ahead of 2020<sup>’</sup>s previous record for this period (European Credit Weekly, Jun 7).

## Currencies

Politics, positioning, payrolls, pain & Powell. EM elections dominated FX this week and spilled into G10 via deleveraging; proxies suggest one-fifth of carry positions may have been unwound. US payrolls challenge perceptions of fading US exceptionalism for USD. BoC kept the door open for more policy divergence, but rates moves and short CAD FX positioning are advanced and risk/reward to shorts has decreased.

Trade recommendations: Keeping pro-cyclical and USD positive exposure light into another event risk–heavy week. Long USD vs EUR, CNH. Long AUD vs JPY, CHF. Short CHF vs USD, EUR. Hit profit-stop on short CAD/NOK. Stay MW in EM FX overall, with OW in Latam and EMEA EM, and UW EM Asia (FXMW, Jun 7).

## Commodities

OPEC+’s decision to return oil supply to the market later this year has alarmed traders. However, a number of key OPEC producers are already pumping above their assigned quotas, and many OPEC members are operating at close to full capacity. The summer inventory draws should be enough to get Brent back into the high \$80-90 range by September. But OPEC<sup>’</sup>s intention to produce more might have a psychological effect on consumers<sup>’</sup> willingness to replenish inventories. Pressure on prices could build in 2025 as supply outside of OPEC rises and demand slows (Oil Markets Weekly, Jun 6 ).

The global Natgas balance tightens as Russian supply uncertainty rises, not only through Ukraine but also through TurkStream. The potential for this supply side outage to Europe is more than enough to require some sort of price premium to be injected in TTF price. We revised our near-term TTF price forecast from 20 to 40 EUR/MWh for 3Q24. We are also raising our outlook for TTF price this winter with 4Q24 and 1Q25 prices now expected to average 50 EUR/MWh and our 2025 price outlook to 38.75 EUR/MWh (European Natural Gas, Jun 3).

Seasonal contract expirations drive open interest value off YTD highs. Global commodity markets experienced significant outflows concentrated in gold, crude oil, and natural gas markets, while the agri market registered modest inflows over the week. We see strong oil demand (just not for crude) for \$10/bbl upside by September. The resilience of gold prices

above \$2,300/oz has kept investor positioning in the COMEX Gold futures contract strong, while aluminum prices remained resilient above the \$2,700/mt despite a sharper pullback in copper prices. In the upcoming season, Black Sea wheat export availability and Ukrainian corn export availability are projected to decline. We flag the continuation of short covering risks across grain and sugar markets. Price momentum generally declined across all the commodity markets (Commodity Market Positioning & Flows, Jun 3).

## US: Looking at both sides of the interest income ledger

• In the aggregate, interest paid always equals interest received...

• ...but governments, households, and nonfinancial businesses are usually net payers...

• ...while financial businesses and the foreign sector are net receivers

• These interest income flows show no systematic reaction to Fed hiking cycles

• Interest income effects are unlikely to explain the unusual reaction of the economy to higher rates

Higher interest rates can operate on the economy through income effects. These income effects from higher rates for lenders are favorable, while those for borrowers are adverse. When thinking about the aggregate effects of interest income flows, one always needs to keep in mind that for the economy as a whole interest paid equals interest received. On a moment<sup>’</sup>s reflection this is obvious, but <sup>‘</sup>fast thinking<sup>’</sup> in economics can lead to some sloppy outcomes.

Given this economy-wide accounting identity, interest income effects are only meaningful when the marginal propensity to consume (MPC) of interest receivers is materially different from that of interest payers. This note investigates that idea. To preview the conclusion, interest income flows may boost aggregate demand by a tenth or two of GDP, which isn<sup>’</sup>t too different from history.

Last week the BEA published the annual estimates of interest paid and received by sector, benchmarked through 2022, the year rate hikes began. Though in many cases we have timelier data. The focus of this note will be monetary interest, which is the everyday concept of the flow of dollars from borrowers to lenders. We do not look at imputed interest, which is how much interest lenders notionally give up to receive things like free checking, ATM withdrawals, etc. This imaginary, imputed interest is large, accounting for over half of the interest income in the personal income report.

We start with the household sector, which includes owner-occupiers (and hence their mortgage interest payments). The household sector is one of the few to experience a secular change in its net interest income position, becoming a net payer in the early <sup>‘</sup>90s (Figure 1) and remaining a net payer of around 0.4% of GDP in 2022.

Figure 1: Household interest income % of GDP, triangles are JPM 1Q24 estimate
![](images/bddc52968e113cda1d97c64408b0a92e66fbd07e84b4d9038490661e1611ce8d.jpg)

Based on affiliated data from the BEA, we estimate this widened to a net paying position of 0.9% of GDP in 1Q24. While this would suggest Fed hikes hurt household income, prior hiking cycles don<sup>’</sup>t display a clear pattern.

Turning to the nonfinancial corporate sector (Figure 2), this sector has always been a net payer. Based on the most recent profits data, it appears that net payments narrowed from 1.5% of GDP in 2022 to 1.0% in 1Q24, as earnings on corporate cash grew quicker than payments on corporate debt.

Figure 2: Interest income, nonfinancial corporations % of GDP, triangles are JPM 1Q24 estimate
![](images/8e2655321d8b15bd8c6314f86b8089de4743db85518315b841d90e4a6f909cd8.jpg)

Three points should be kept in mind. First, in the years after benchmarking (2022), these net payments are estimated as a residual of every other sectors<sup>’</sup> net payments, thus inheriting all their estimation errors. Second, the fun part of the cycle is probably over for this sector. Income receipts are generally from short duration assets. With hikes now done, growth in income receipts should stall while longer-duration obligations will slowly reset into higher payments. In fact, over the past two quarters, nonfinancial corporate net interest payments have begun to tick higher. Third, we know from publicly listed firms that most cash is held by large multinationals and that the share of debt that is fixed tends to increase with firm size.

In light of this last point, it is not surprising that privately held businesses are net interest payers as well (Figure 3). While we do not have timelier data on this sector<sup>’</sup>s interest receipts and payments, it is doubtful they have benefited early on in the Fed hiking cycle in the same manner as nonfinancial corporations.

Figure 3: Private business interest income % of GDP
![](images/aeaadf06c9b9e4b08aff86fdb73fdc36ab324df8333b7a307332c901c4719443.jpg)

Turning to financial corporations (Figure 4), they are structural net interest recipients. Moreover, their net receipts have no clear relation to Fed hiking cycles, something we stressed in our post-mortem of last spring<sup>’</sup>s regional banking crisis.

Figure 4: Interest income, financial corporations % of GDP
![](images/ea3c1868ab8277ca70b93470078903e0a0da6c51b77198f06cc89d47919bcead.jpg)

Ever since the US became a net debtor in the 1980s, the foreign sector has been a net interest income recipient (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Interest income, rest of the world % of GDP
![](images/8ddb3ea5f9721eb7a1f8141a4f3608b6bc0caf48a4977862c6e5f3387eb03a83.jpg)
Lastly, the government sector is a large and growing net interest payer (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Interest income, government sector % of GDP, triangles are JPM 1Q24 estimate
![](images/a92e26521e2d1668c352a8639c98cc687a8b2357b09798c4c0a484ec35c3a9de.jpg)

Net payments by the government sector reached 2.9% of GDP in 2022 and appear to have increased another 1.0%-point by 1Q24. Probably a tenth or two of this increase leaked abroad. Perhaps half of it found its way into nonfinancial corporates. The other 0.3-0.4%-point is unaccounted for but likely went to financial corporations.

The government is a low MPC interest payer, perhaps as low as zero. So is the foreign sector. The CBO has estimated the MPC of the business sector at 0.2, though this should be smaller for larger corporations. All in, we think the income effects have boosted aggregate demand by at most 0.2% of GDP, though probably less. This doesn<sup>’</sup>t account for the increase in net interest payments by the household sector. In any case, the flows of interest income don<sup>’</sup>t appear sufficient to explain the resilience of the economy in this cycle.

Finally, this tiny number (a tenth or two of GDP) doesn<sup>’</sup>t appear anomalous to this cycle; it is hard to see consistently large or small interest income effects associated with rate hike cycles. The potency of rate hikes comes from the various other channels of monetary policy transmission—asset price, exchange rate, user cost, etc.

## BoE: Reasons not to stop active gilt sales

BoE may slow active sales from October to smooth through a bump higher in maturities

But a stop in sales would appear inconsistent with the BoE’s stated objective of gradualism in the process

• We illustrate this using a model optimized to minimize volatility in both active sales and overall QT

• A stop would have fiscal repercussions the BoE would probably rather avoid

Later this year the BoE will have to make a choice on the pace of QT. Owing to a spike higher in maturing gilts, the Bank will either have to allow an increase in the annual pace from £100bn or else abruptly slow the pace of active sales from £50bn to around £13bn. There are good reasons for the BoE to avoid the latter. We look for a more gradual slowing in active sales to around £30bn, as the BoE seeks to offer a degree of smoothing in both active sales and overall QT. Our expectation would result in the overall pace of gilt reduction stepping up to around £120bn over the four quarters from 4Q.

Several clients we have spoken to are expecting a complete stop in active sales, which would see the overall QT pace slow to below £90bn. Based on the way the BoE has described how it would approach QT decisions, it is hard to understand why it would choose this option. Some have suggested the BoE will be uncomfortable in having interest rate and balance sheet policy pulling in opposite directions, while others have argued there would be a fiscal advantage from stopping sales. We expect the BoE will push back on both arguments, consistent with the MPC<sup>’</sup>s stated objective to ensure continuity in the process where possible. We use a model to illustrate how a stop in gilt sales would be inconsistent with this objective.

The BoE has indicated a preference to continue unwinding its gilt portfolio even after bank reserves return back to equilibrium levels. Stopping now would either mean restarting again the following year, hence adding unnecessary volatility, or accepting a much slower pace of gilt reduction in general. Sales have been digested well by markets so far, and the BoE is uncomfortable being associated with the interest rate risk from a large gilt portfolio for longer than is necessary. It makes sense, in our view, to seize every viable opportunity to downsize.

Increasing QT would not have a material impact on the ultimate lifetime losses the government has agreed to pick up, but stopping sales would confer a small near-term fiscal advantage to the next government due to the arbitrary nature in which the fiscal rules are currently defined. We note the optics around this for an independent central bank are not good, and could make it harder for the BoE to start selling gilts again. Even with a slowing in sales, the BoE should use forward guidance to signal this will likely be temporary. This would be acknowledged by the OBR and remove the link to measured fiscal headroom.

## The QT story to date

The BoE<sup>’</sup>s balance sheet reached a peak of £1.1tn after its pandemic related asset purchases, with gilt holdings and reserves around the £900bn mark. The BoE began QT in 2022 and at an annual pace of £80bn from October, comprising active sales of £45bn and a passive unwind through maturing gilts worth £35bn. The BoE assessed all was working well and took advantage of a step up in maturities from October 2023 to increase the overall pace to £100bn. The balance sheet is now closer to the £900bn mark, with gilts and reserves near £700bn.

The BoE has highlighted the distinction between gilts on the asset side of its balance sheet and reserves on the liabilities side. Governor Bailey recently cited BoE surveys suggesting an equilibrium level of reserves desired by banks that lies within a £345-490bn range, much higher than before the use of QE due to a higher demand for liquidity. Based on the current trajectory, this would imply equilibrium reserves could be achieved at some point between late 2025 and early 2027.

The BoE intends to allow its balance sheet to stabilize at this level. It won<sup>’</sup>t know in advance exactly where that level is, and in any case has a preference to continue in unwinding its gilt portfolio after reserves - and hence overall balance sheet size - have normalized. To prevent an unwanted spike in money market rates it has set up a short-term one week borrowing facility (STR). This is accessible by banks at Bank Rate with pre-cleared gilt collateral, and is available at full allotment. This will ensure banks have access to the level of reserves they require, and would be similar to the way the system used to work before QE.

![](images/348050b02cf187b24f9a26da156cf6b0ee8af5691143ae6f7ca240169a7ad44b.jpg)

Recently STR usage has increased, and some have suggested this is a signal that the current level of reserves may already be close to the equilibrium. But the BoE has recently argued against this. Figure 1 shows how we might expect STR usage to evolve based on the equilibrium level of reserves suggested by Bailey. The STR will increase over time, substituting for the unwind of gilts on the balance sheet and keeping reserves close to their equilibrium.

It looks very likely that the BoE will continue unwinding its gilt portfolio after the balance sheet stabilizes. We assume a step down in active gilt sales to just under £30bn from this October, with overall QT at £120bn. When then assume an annual pace of £40-50bn in subsequent years which leaves overall QT running at an average pace of £80-90bn per year (Figure 2). This would leave gilt holdings of around £200bn on the balance sheet by 2029. With repos under the STR running at just over £300bn around the same time, this would still leave gilts accounting for some 40% of the two. Stopping sales and shifting just to passive QT would leave gilts accounting for closer to 80%. As we discuss below, this would seem inconsistent with the BoE<sup>’</sup>s preference to rely more heavily on STRs over time.

Figure 2: Gilts on BoE balance sheet
![](images/1f0931e6b8e39502ce20dbe033f5a149b31a407c01a206802ff3f8267884fbe1.jpg)

## Interaction with fiscal policy

BoE gilt holdings result in an interest rate risk for the Bank. There is a mismatch between the fixed rate assets held by the BoE (gilts) and its variable rate liabilities (central bank reserves). When rates were low this generated profits worth a cumulative £124bn, and under current arrangements these profits were transferred to the government at its own request. But as rates have risen there have been interest losses from the BoE<sup>’</sup>s portfolio. In addition, the BoE is also selling gilts back at a lower price than it purchased them at due to the rise in interest rates. This creates additional capital losses. Losses are expected to outweigh previous profits with total expected losses in the region of £130bn or around 3% of GDP by 2033. A government indemnity arrangement means interest losses influence the recorded fiscal deficit, while both interest and capital losses affect recorded public debt. Other central banks instead maintain the losses on their balance sheet, and use seigniorage revenue to pay it off over time. But seigniorage revenue in the UK is already transferred to the government.

A popular argument is that the BoE could reduce capital losses if it stopped selling back gilts, and instead chose to shrink its balance sheet by stopping reinvestments alone. But this would mean gilts stay on the balance sheet for longer. And based on current market rate expectations, the more prolonged period of flow losses would mean the public purse is not significantly better off in the long run. The BoE previously estimated that the NPV losses from alternative QT strategies lie within a range of £60-90bn (2-3% of GDP). A strategy that relies solely on a passive gilt unwind could result in losses at the lower end of this range, but the difference is small compared to the uncertainty about interest rates. If interest rates came down to the BoE<sup>’</sup>s 2-3% estimate of the long run equilibrium rate, instead of running in line with market rate expectations of around 3.5-4%, this would be worth £40bn or 1-2% of GDP.

Where a shift in strategy would make a difference is over shorter term horizons. A stop in active sales now would reduce the annual losses by around £10bn over the next decade, the horizon over which the fiscal rules will apply to the next government. The rules state the debt ratio should be falling at the end of a five year rolling window, so in theory this could create space for an additional fiscal easing worth 0.3% of GDP.

## Reasons not to stop active sales

Overall, we see several reasons for the BoE not to stop active sales. The first three related to independence and BoE credibility over the use of QE, while the latter three relate to market functioning and operational concerns:

Avoiding an influence on fiscal headroom. While the BoE<sup>’</sup>s remit states that it should support the government<sup>’</sup>s economic policy, this is defined as “responsible fiscal policy<sup>”</sup> and comes second to maintaining price stability. The fiscal advantage would be small, but without an obvious monetary policy motive would further politicize QT decisions and undermine BoE independence. Bailey recently stated that the “scale and pace of the reduction in the stock of assets in the APF as part of QT are chosen solely to meet the MPC<sup>’</sup>s policy objectives. The APF is indemnified by HM Treasury to ensure that the MPC can make its decisions in this way, in line with its statutory objectives and the remit given to it by the Government.

Reducing interest rate risk for the BoE. Bailey recently drew attention to the interest rate associated with its gilt holdings as alluded to above. Although the BoE has a government indemnity, the current setup in the UK means

BoE interest rate decisions have at times come under political scrutiny in a way the Bank would like to avoid. Bailey stated that “QT will enter a new phase where it changes the mix of assets on the central bank balance sheet but not the level of central bank reserves<sup>”</sup>. It has yet to commit to any particular mix, but its preference lies in shorter term repos which Bailey stated have “much appeal<sup>”</sup> and “score very well against all of our principles<sup>”</sup>.

Opportunistic progress on QE headroom. Bailey recently appeared to define available QE headroom in terms of where reserves are relative to their equilibrium. But the BoE has also previously defined it in terms of concentration constraints in the gilt market. An ongoing fiscal deficit means this has never really been a binding constraint, but there are optical considerations associated with buying gilts in size and then not fully unwinding the process. To highlight independence from fiscal policy and retain credibility with the option of undertaking purchases again in the future, there is an incentive for the BoE to reduce gilt holdings where feasible.

No conflict with gradual rate cuts. Some have suggested QT is incompatible with interest rate cuts. But Ramsden stated last year that the BoE<sup>’</sup>s experience with QE suggested a QT impact “on activity and inflation of less than 0.2% and 0.1pp respectively<sup>”</sup>. This would be worth less than 25bps on rates. The minutes of the August 2022 MPC meeting stated that QT would be amended only “if potential movements in Bank Rate alone were judged insufficient to meet the inflation target<sup>”</sup>. With rates starting at 5.25% it<sup>’</sup>s hard to argue that QT should be changed due to the easing cycle.

Market is digesting QT well so far. The BoE has stated it would suspend sales if they disrupted the functioning of financial markets. Aside from the 2022 mini-Budget episode, the BoE concluded last year that QT has gone well with “no evidence of a negative impact...on market functioning<sup>”</sup>. Deputy Governor Ramsden in 2023 indicated that the £80bn pace of overall QT at the time might be worth less than 10bps on ten year gilt yields.

Ensuring continuity. Another of the BoE<sup>’</sup>s key QT principles is to unwind the gilt portfolio in a “gradual and predictable manner<sup>”</sup> that does not disrupt the functioning of financial markets. The MPC notes that while its main focus is on the reduction of the total gilt stock, i.e. comprising maturing gilts and sales, and that the “natural variation in maturities may also lead to variation in the pace of sales over time<sup>”</sup>. But it also emphasizes that the MPC “places some weight on continuity in sales<sup>”</sup>.

## Smoothing QT by constrained optimization

One way for the BoE to demonstrate it is taking decisions on gilt sales to smooth the adjustment rather than political considerations, for example, would be for it to use some kind of automated process. We have set up a simple example below that derives a path for QT by optimizing a loss function that penalizes yearly volatility. We define QT as the sum of active sales and maturities:

$$
\mathrm { Q T } _ { \mathrm { t } } { = } \mathrm { S a l e s } _ { \mathrm { t } } { + } \mathrm { M a t u r i t i e s } _ { \mathrm { t } }
$$

The future path of maturing gilts is known in advance, and so overall QT will be determined by decisions on active sales each year by the BoE. But the loss function penalizes year on year volatility in both active gilt sales and total QT:

$$
\mathrm { L o s s } _ { \mathrm { t } } = \mathrm { a } ^ { \ast } ( \mathrm { Q T } _ { \mathrm { t } } - \mathrm { Q T } _ { \mathrm { t - l } } ) ^ { 2 } + ( \mathrm { 1 - \mathrm { a } } ) ^ { \ast } ( \mathrm { S a l e s } _ { \mathrm { t } } - \mathrm { S a l e s } _ { \mathrm { t - l } } ) ^ { 2 }
$$

where α is a weight that represents the relative importance of minimizing the overall QT path versus sales. We assume no reinvestments and no asset purchases. Over a multi-year period of T years, the total loss will therefore be:

Total Loss = Σ<sup>T</sup><sub>t=1</sub> Loss<sub>t</sub>

Figure 3 below shows the path of active sales and overall QT that would minimize the loss defined above over the period 2024-2029. The base case uses an alpha of 0.5, but the shaded area shows the results under the assumptions of values of 0 and 1. If the BoE were to suspend sales for a year, this would mark a significant departure from the objective of providing gradualism and predictability in the process.

Figure 3: An optimal QT path based around gradualism
![](images/56f1c54701238bea0c0cbb4c203390a3fbba756ca50806af56b8d422f02b5fd7.jpg)

The OBR<sup>’</sup>s current assumption that sales remain at a pace of £48bn per year, and the projections we present in Figures 1 and 2, are both within the shaded areas shown in Figure 3. There may be several legitimate reasons for the BoE to deviate from this path, including operational considerations, market functioning concerns or a desire to deliver a higher level of gilts on the balance sheet than we assume. But none of these appears dominant at the moment.

## Africa: Leaving stress behind

• African economies are on the mend after a tumultuous prior four years...

• … with growth rebounding, inflation receding, fiscal and external balances improving

Risks are tilted to the downside from commodity price and climatic shocks and a persistent funding squeeze

• For the defaulted names, we expect an end to the debt restructuring for Ghana and Zambia this year

African economies have endured a turbulent four years – grappling with persistent external shocks emanating from the pandemic, followed by Russia<sup>’</sup>s invasion of Ukraine, with the consequent shock to commodity prices, and a sustained period of global financial tightening. Policy makers in the region also reacted strongly, with the most accelerated pace of monetary tightening on record to tame very high inflation. As external markets remained shut for the last two years, funding squeeze pressures arose with large debt roll-over risks. Authorities have had to lower primary deficits, increase reliance on domestic borrowing and engage new multilateral sources of financing to bridge both budget and external gaps.

As financing conditions have started to ease somewhat, external bond markets have re-opened with eurobond issuances from Cote d<sup>’</sup>Ivoire, Benin and Kenya in Q1. We expect more African sovereign bond issuance later this year, which could be buoyed by further narrowing of bond spreads in the expectation of global financial conditions easing further in 2H24. Furthermore, foreign portfolio inflows into domestic markets have also resumed with renewed interest in the carry trades of Egypt, Kenya and, to a lesser extent, Nigeria. This should continue to help to rebuild FX buffers and ease currency pressures.

While we expect a firm disinflation path in 2H24, we think central banks in the region are likely to keep nominal rates elevated, allowing for a sustained period of high ex-post real rates until inflation falls sustainably towards target. Anchoring inflation expectations and staving off FX pressures will also be a key consideration for central banks to keep rates high for longer. In a scenario where US Fed cuts are very limited, external financing of budget deficits from market and commercial sources will prove more expensive and difficult. In that event, keeping relatively high nominal rates could help attract foreign portfolio flows, as already seen this year

Figure 1: Inflation deviation from central bank target
![](images/2d14bc55999c05e3cf23d518cff09ac7dc7938bc75e75bb91018db710d348b4f.jpg)
Source: Central Banks and J.P. Morgan

The region remains quite vulnerable to global shocks, particularly from commodities, as most economies remain very commodity dependent. However, climatic shocks appear more pertinent and could have an impact on inflation and fiscal and external balances. The EL Niño weather phenomenon is delivering droughts to the southern parts of the region, while causing floods in eastern Africa. This could present another layer of complication in the ongoing funding squeeze as financing gaps grow.

Multilateral financing along with international grants could provide some cushion, but authorities will have to continue to embark on fiscal and structural reforms to spur economic growth, lower fiscal balances and stabilise debt over the medium term. For the defaulted names, we see some light at the end of the tunnel as we expect the conclusion of the external debt restructuring in Zambia and Ghana this year.

## Growth recovery rewards diversity

The divergence in growth trends between commodity-dependent economies and the more diversified ones has been more entrenched since the initial oil price collapse in 2015. As Figure 2 shows, commodity-dependent economies like Nigeria, Angola and Gabon have seen much slower cumulative growth since 2019 than the more diversified/less commodity-dependent ones. While the commodity-dependent economies should start to see slightly improved growth dynamics from this year onwards, we think the divergence will persist with non-resource dependent economies still logging higher growth rates.

In 2024, Senegal will likely be the one of the fastest growing economies in the region, owing to new oil and gas projects commencing. Over the medium term, we expect private consumption and investment to drive growth. While the former should be helped by a moderation in inflation, authorities will need to embark on structural reforms and enact domestic policies that will attract investments into key sectors of the economies.

Figure 2: Economic growth trends
![](images/f005c1cca906bb981afc01a678f5afa5fc1f0ecada11198322ba089dc0a3bbf3.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, IMF, J.P. Morgan \* Resource intensive countries
Source: IMF, Haver Analytics, J.P. Morgan

Figure 4: Headline inflation
![](images/47a6897c8179753cd49bd982c3a902a69925da71fad474b1ecb610492d636e9d.jpg)

## Inflation sticky on the way down

While headline inflation in the region has not decelerated at the pace being witnessed in most other emerging market and frontier economies, we think it has already passed its peak in most countries and is close to peaking in others. Two factors have been largely responsible for the stickiness: renewed currency pressures and persistent food price inflation. These factors largely explain the relatively strong inflation momentum still being witnessed in Egypt, Nigeria, Angola and Zambia. For Nigeria and Angola more specifically, some idiosyncratic factors are also at play, as both authorities have embarked on some form of fiscal reforms via fuel subsidy removals over the last year, and that has pushed the general level of prices higher. A firm pace of disinflation is already underway in Cote d<sup>’</sup>Ivoire and Senegal and, to an extent, Kenya, although recent flooding events create upside risks to the inflation path.

![](images/8e200e969a6d3ae3249fa88f113ed19245c6c7ef5e95fc4308dd4f24cce52a4b.jpg)
Figure 3: Headline inflation in EM Edge regions

More broadly, we expect headline inflation to decelerate in the region during 2H24, thanks in large part to base effects and abating currency pressures, amid the potential for increased FX inflows. That said, monetary policy authorities are likely to sustain a tight monetary policy stance for longer in a bid to lower inflation more firmly towards target. As such, we only see scope for rate cuts in Ghana and Kenya this year. The broader region, particularly those countries with ensuing currency pressures, is likely to keep nominal rates and ex post real rates higher for longer.

## External buffers should improve further

Gross FX reserves in the region generally continued to improve over the last few months (but still lower than their levels two years ago, Figure 5), supported not only by improvements in current accounts, but also by easier global financial conditions and better access to funding. On the latter, external issuances have been less pronounced in the region but there have been increased inflows into local markets. The WAEMU region is where FX reserves have persistently deteriorated in the last two years, amid wider current and fiscal account deficits, fixed exchange rates and large fiscal funding. However, we still expect some external issuances from that region later in the year and prospects of increased oil and gas flows from Senegal and Niger should improve the outlook.

Nigeria continues to face downward pressure because of the drag from a negative errors-adjusted current account position. Besides that, over the recent period, the CBN has faced specific shocks that required FX intervention, while it also aimed to reduce expensive US dollar liabilities. We think that external issuance, along with further non-resident inflows into local bonds, should put FX reserves on an uptrend, but risks are clearly to the downside, in our view.

Figure 5: Change in FX reserve stock
![](images/aabe0e3778b7f39a39ca4eead98e66bdcf61b7595e5380c206ffc10dfea3f4c8.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, IMF J.P. Morgan \*Latest data is Aug 23

Figure 6: Gross reserves import cover ratio Ratio, 12m avg. imports of g&s
![](images/024939d7bd3fc86f15108f01c6855cbf8da71378702475e7c7be0be0fc4be960.jpg)
Source: National statistics, IMF, J.P. Morgan

## The positive fiscal story

African economies have recorded improvements in primary balances in recent years. Gains were significant last year and are likely to continue in 2024 . Yet, the reasons behind the improvements vary somewhat between countries. While the relatively high oil price environment, post Covid, has been instrumental in improved primary balances in Angola and Nigeria, revenue-led fiscal consolidation drives backed by IMF programmes have been key for Kenya, Ghana, Zambia and Egypt. The pace of consolidation has been quickest for Ghana, which, after suspending interest payments and domestic debt restructuring, has been able to lower its overall fiscal deficit from a record high 17.4% in 2020 to 4.6% in 2023. Authorities have also managed to curb some non-essential spending and reduce the stock of untargeted subsidies in Kenya, Angola, Nigeria and, to a lesser extent, Egypt.

Figure 7: Primary balances in EM Edge, EMX and US
![](images/a0e1b40b2b4f5f319563f5293942caaf8fa7d2f5ade3d2cb7ebfeba496634543.jpg)

That said, the higher interest burden should remain a source of fiscal pressure for most of these economies this year, following an accelerated pace of monetary tightening and amid a high stock of short-term domestic debt; Egypt and Nigeria rank highly in this regard. Should the authorities be able to maintain the current reform path and consistently improve primary balances, there should be much scope to bring debt levels down more sustainably over the medium term.

## United States

• Job growth still chugging along as nonfarm employment increased by 272,000 in May...

• ...but the unemployment rate ticked up to 4.0%, and job openings moved lower in April

• In next week<sup>’</sup>s May CPI release, we look for another firm core reading of 0.33%

• We expect a Fed on hold next week and now look for a first hike in November

Ahead of next week<sup>’</sup>s FOMC meeting this week brought a checkup on the labor market. The biggest news was the May jobs report: employment growth rebounded smartly last month, with nonfarm jobs up 272,000 (prior months revised down 15,000 on net). Pay growth also firmed again as average hourly earnings rose 0.4%, taking the year-ago increase to 4.1%. The average workweek was unchanged at 34.3 hours.

Meanwhile, the unemployment rate ticked up to 4.0%, the highest since January 2022, and most other metrics from the household survey were less favorable or outright weak. The household measure of employment contracted by 408,000, unemployment increased 157,000, and the participation rate slipped two ticks to 62.5%. Over the past 12 months the unemployment rate is up 0.4%-points, and during the same period nonfarm employment growth averaged 230,000 jobs per month. The confluence of these two facts points to (i) the importance of immigration in boosting trend, or breakeven, job growth, and (ii) the fact that the monthly establishment survey is likely overstating the pace of job growth.

Sending a message more akin to the household report, the April JOLTS report pointed to a continuing moderation in job openings, and the vacancy-to-unemployment ratio came down to 1.2, from a peak of 2.0 earlier in the cycle. Likewise, the quits rate remains lower than earlier in the cycle, when workers were job-hopping more in search of higher wages.

After the May FOMC meeting, when the unemployment rate was 3.8%, Chair Powell said “a couple of tenths [increase] in the unemployment rate<sup>”</sup> would not be sufficient to ease as he would need to see “broader<sup>”</sup> weakening. Last month<sup>’</sup>s perky job growth and persistent strength in wage growth are still very far from meeting that broader test, and a July ease now looks highly unlikely. It<sup>’</sup>s possible that the weakness in the household survey broadens out in the three employment reports between now and the September FOMC, but given the recent momentum in job growth we think it may take the five reports available between now and the November FOMC meeting to meet the test. We now see the first ease in November, followed by a quarterly cadence of rate cuts next year.

## Labor market still hot to the touch

In the details of the establishment survey, health care again led job growth, adding 84,000 jobs in May. After cooling in April, government employment rose 43,000 last month. While these industries led the way, job growth was broad based with 63.4% of industries increasing employment, the strongest diffusion index reading in over a year (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Private industry diffusion index: 1-month
![](images/b32f6dda7997a55cd50b59d5964a300d53a65b7f7dad6ca557cb0ca63d62dd7d.jpg)

The average workweek was unchanged near the low end of its recent range at 34.3 hours (34.296 hours to higher precision). The strength in headline earnings was mirrored in the production and non-supervisory worker series, where average hourly earnings increased 0.5% last month and the year-ago gain ticked up to 4.2% (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Average hourly earnings %ch, over year-ago
![](images/5597278c5815d43d362106d520f08ac8d6732a276eaa4da6458a1d669aa7ef72.jpg)

## Household puzzles

Turning to the household survey, some of last month<sup>’</sup>s rise in the unemployment rate was due to higher teen unemployment, though the 20+ rate also increased a tick to 3.6%, versus a cycle low of 3.2%. The 376,000 year-ago increase in household employment still contrasts markedly with the 2.786 million rise in the establishment survey (Figure 3). Moreover, the payroll concept-adjusted measure looks even weaker, down 456,000 last month and up only 216,000 over the last year. Mirroring quits in the JOLTS report, job leavers continues to decline to a near three-year low while job losers constitute a larger share of the unemployed (Figure 4).

Figure 3: Employment growth

000s, over year-ago

![](images/d1866863fc3109bc5fc84e40e9ba42bed9931b47d8f0d17459234fb6e3d7634a.jpg)

Figure 4: Job leavers to job losers Ratio
![](images/fe6c162510d05bdad4877453bee1af5d2c72f027bb1719500cfe2b6d113bf5ce.jpg)
Source: BLS, J.P. Morgan

In contrast to the headline participation rate number, primeage (25-54) participation ticked up last month to a cycle high 83.6%, and prime-age female participation hit an all-time high of 78.1%. The gains were offset by big declines in the over 55 participation rate in May (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Labor force participation rate
![](images/ffe810f282f3f94a23b58fa914b8c7733356c82560dd5a8def4b028600750828.jpg)

## Job openings resumed their decline

Job openings declined by 296,000, and downward revisions to prior months brought the level down to 8.06 million, the lowest since February 2021. Chair Powell frequently references the vacancy-to-unemployment ratio as a measure of labor market tightness, and in April this ratio moved down further to 1.24. This ratio peaked at 2.0 earlier in this cycle, and a reading in the range of 1.0-1.2 is considered consistent with a healthy balanced labor market.

Figure 6: Vacancy-to-unemployment ratio
![](images/727b37a2748be20a841cb65f7b00acffe8427cdf6eacf33a07f5d7e892a3bb6c.jpg)

## CPI and Fed ahead

The May CPI report will be released the morning of next week<sup>’</sup>s FOMC meeting. We look for a relatively firm 0.33% increase in core CPI inflation that keeps year-ago inflation unchanged at 3.6%. In the details, we look for OER and rents to remain elevated, alongside some continued strength in medical care and a sizable reversal in used vehicle prices growth from April. While we continue to expect further cooling in inflation this year—and notably the FOMC is focused on PCE inflation, which is running below CPI—the current pace may leave various participants still concerned about a lack of further progress.

Next week the Committee is almost certain to leave the target range unchanged at 5.25-5.5%. The big question is the median 2024 dot. We think it will show two eases this year, down from three at the March FOMC meeting. With almost half the Committee expecting two or fewer rate cuts in March, this is a close call and the median could easily go to one if Chair Powell and the leadership are so minded. For both 2025 and 2026 we think the median remains at three eases, while the longer-run median dot could drift up to 2.75% as several Fed speakers have opined in recent months that the neutral funds rate is likely moving higher.

As for the post-meeting statement, the description of activity growth could be moderated given recent data. And next Wednesday<sup>’</sup>s CPI report could influence how they tweak the inflation language. At the press conference, we expect Chair Powell will express confidence that the economy is still on a path toward a sustained recovery with inflation heading toward two percent—and that they can remain patient as they expect to gradually gain more confidence in that outlook.

We forecast that the consumer price index (CPI) rose 0.2% in May, with the year-ago inflation rate holding steady at 3.4%. We look for a 0.1% increase in food prices, in line with the relatively soft growth in recent months, and a -1.5% drop in energy prices on the month led by a -3.4% monthly decline in gasoline prices. Away from food and energy, we are looking for a firmer core CPI reading, however, with a 0.33% increase in May. If realized, this print will be strong enough to hold the year-ago core CPI inflation rate steady at 3.6%.

Shelter is the largest piece of the CPI index and is closely watched for signs of easing. We look for only modest cooling from the still-firm April readings, despite signs of prices fading in certain rental measures. Specifically, we look for a 0.40% rise in Owners<sup>’</sup> Equivalent Rent and a 0.36% rise in tenant<sup>’</sup>s rent, which are similar to the paces they<sup>’</sup>ve run in recent months. Elsewhere within the shelter component, after seasonal adjustment industry figures point to a second month of moderate cooling in lodging. We forecast the lodging CPI slipped -0.1% in May.

One of the larger shifts in CPI components is expected to occur in vehicle prices in May. Most significantly, based on industry data we look for used vehicle prices to rise a sharp 1.5% last month, more than reversing the decline recorded for April. We also anticipate more modest firming in new vehicle prices, up 0.2%, after contracting in six of the last seven months. In other transportation-related news, we look for a flat reading in recently volatile public transportation prices.

Communication prices are expected to resume their downward trend in May after postage stamp prices pushed up this measure earlier in the year—although additional increases have been approved to take effect in July. While medical insurance prices took a step down in April and should continue to run at a more moderate pace than earlier in the year, continued firmness in other parts of the medical CPI leave us looking for a 0.4% rise in this component in May. After several months of notable price increases, we look for the apparel CPI to tick down in May.

1. Payroll survey week

We think that initial claims for jobless benefits declined by 4,000 to 225,000 during the week ending June 8. Claims data can be noisy during the weeks around holidays like the Memorial Day, and we think some of that noise might still be in place for the week ending June 8. The broader set of labor market variables are consistent with this relatively stable level of claims at historically low levels.

We estimate that the producer price index (PPI) inched up 0.1% in May, a deceleration from a strong April print. This reading would raise the year-ago PPI inflation rate to a 14- month high of 2.5%. Further declines in individual energy prices, particularly gasoline, should lead to a -1.6% drop in the energy PPI in May, reversing some of the jump in April. Meanwhile, the food PPI should jump 0.6% after a large decline in April. Away from food and energy, we look for core PPI prices to have risen 0.2% in May, which is potentially more in line with the underlying pace of core producer prices after several volatile monthly readings. As such, we expect the year-ago core PPI inflation rate to remain at 2.4% in May.

We expect the import price index fell -0.2% in May as recent weakness in fuel prices globally likely pushed the aggregate import price index lower. Away from fuels, we believe the import price index rose 0.1% in May. We anticipate that the pace of growth in food prices slowed substantially last month after a run of strong monthly prints since the start of this year. Outside of food and fuels, we expect mixed price changes on the month with broad-based cooling relative to last month. That said, we look for year-ago growth rates for the main import price indexes to have moved up slightly relative to April<sup>’</sup>s readings, suggesting that imported sources of disinflation for goods prices may be mostly behind us.

We look for the University of Michigan consumer sentiment index to have risen 3.9-pts to 73.0 in the preliminary June report. This gauge of consumer attitudes has been generally trending higher since hitting a cycle low in mid 2022, albeit in a rather choppy manner. The recent gains in equity markets and the decline in gasoline prices, as well as the still-strong employment growth, should help to boost sentiment and push the index back up moderately this month. We will also keep a close eye on the inflation expectations in the survey as they have moved up again recently.

The two main monthly surveys of manufacturers—the ISM and the PMI—gave conflicting signals for the near-term health of the manufacturing sector, although both have drifted upward since late last year. But whereas the PMI now stands comfortably above its level at the start of the year, the ISM is down from recent highs and only slightly above where it started 2024—with a sizable reversion in new orders back to more depressed levels seen last year. The broad trend in manufacturing output has been mostly sideways in the industrial production data, and averaging through these reports suggests that trend is likely to continue for now. In particular, ordersto-inventory ratios in both surveys have slipped back below one in recent months, which suggests muted momentum for output.

The May ISM manufacturing composite index fell 0.5-pts to 48.7, disappointing expectations. The details were generally weaker across the board, with the employment index being a notable exception as it rose to its highest reading since August 2022. By contrast, the production index fell 1.1-pts to 50.2 while the new orders index took a big step down (-3.7-pts) to 45.4, a 12-month low. Meanwhile, the composite manufacturing PMI for May was revised up to 51.3 from the earlier flash release of 50.9, which represents a 1.3-pts gain from the value recorded in April. Although still below its recent high in February, it remains consistent with a choppy upward trend in the manufacturing sector over roughly the past year. In the details, the output PMI improved to 53.0

from 51.1 in April; new orders, employment, and future output all also posted increases last month.

Consistent with the broad upward trend in both surveys<sup>’</sup> pricing components, goods price data have recently firmed somewhat after deflating outright during the second half of last year. Falling prices in China and a strengthening US dollar should exert some downward pressure on goods prices, whereas recent jumps in shipping costs and continued support on the demand side (which has weakened some in recent spending reports) point to upward pressure. The May ISM prices index ticked lower while import prices moved up in the PMI survey even as output prices were again slightly lower on the month.

Nominal construction spending slid -0.1% in April, disappointing our and consensus expectations for a moderate gain. The details were mixed, however, with a stronger than expected 0.1% rise in private residential construction, largely on single-family new construction and home improvements. Both series were also revised up in March. By contrast, private nonresidential construction spending came in weaker than expected in April, falling -0.3%, with downward revisions to March but a large positive revision to February. Public construction spending also disappointed in April but with notable upward revisions to February and March. On net, these data do not appear to be impactful for our current-quarter GDP tracking of 2.0% q/q saar.

## Motor vehicle sales (Jun 3)

Job openings dropped 296,000 in April to a seasonally adjusted 8.06mn, a larger than expected decline that beat expectations and brought it to its lowest level since February 2021.

The prior month<sup>’</sup>s level was revised down to 8.36mn from the previously reported 8.49mn. This drop brings the openingsto-unemployed ratio, a useful gauge of labor market tightness, to 1.24 for April, down from 1.52 a year ago and very close to its pre-pandemic level of 1.22 in February 2020. As we have argued recently, with this move in job openings, the US labor market seems to be moving along the pre-pandemic Beveridge curve relationship, raising the possibility of a gradually increasing unemployment rate.

Under the hood, it looks like job openings in April declined in health care and social assistance (-204,000), leisure and hospitality (-109,000), state and local government education (-59,000), and information (-43,000), while vacancies increased in professional and business services (+122,000) and private educational services (+50,000). The construction sector<sup>’</sup>s large drop observed in March did not reverse itself, highlighting the softer demand in this sector. Finally, the underlying detail by establishment size continued to indicate relatively stronger labor demand among smaller establishments, which collectively posted more job openings in April relative to March, whereas establishments with more than 50 workers reduced openings.

Beyond openings, hires and total separations changed little and quits stayed relatively flat at 2.2%. The quits rate has been at 2.2% since November and is similar to the levels we have seen at the end of the prior expansion, which marked a fairly tight labor market by historical standards. Relatively stable quit rates over the recent past seem to be consistent with other survey data, such as the mean probability of quits from the New York Fed<sup>’</sup>s Survey of Consumer Expectations. Higher quits rates are generally associated with higher wage gains by workers, and as such, a flat quits rate might be a concern that wage growth could stay elevated, although the relationship has not been very tight lately.

## S&P Global services PMI (US) (Jun 5)

<table><tr><td colspan="3">dcx, sa Flash</td><td rowspan="5">Final May 54.8</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>51.3</td><td>54.8</td></tr><tr><td>Incoming new business</td><td>49.2</td><td>52.0</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>47.7</td><td>49.4</td></tr><tr><td>Business expectations</td><td>65.2</td><td>74.2</td><td>49.4 66.8</td></tr><tr><td>Input prices</td><td>56.0</td><td>57.7</td><td>57.1</td></tr><tr><td>Prices charged</td><td>53.7</td><td>54.0</td><td>54.3</td></tr><tr><td>Backlogs of work</td><td>48.9</td><td>49.8</td><td>49.7</td></tr></table>

The final release of the May services business activity PMI was unchanged from its flash reading of 54.8—representing a strong 3.5-pts rise from the April value. The new business, future activity, and employment components also were unchanged from their flash readings and also improved from April—with the latter still implying a relatively weak labor

market. Input prices were revised slightly higher while output prices were revised slightly lower; both have been moving in a choppy but broadly sideways pattern over the past several months. With this report, the all-industry output PMI improved 3.3-pts to a robust 54.5, its strongest reading in over two years. Together, the May business surveys pour some cold water on the emerging market narrative of a significant slowdown brewing for the US economy, although we do expect some further cooling in growth from the lofty pace recorded during the second half of last year.

## ISM services survey (Jun 5)

<table><tr><td>Sa</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Headline composite</td><td>51.4</td><td>49.4</td><td>51.0 53.8</td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>57.4</td><td>50.9</td><td>61.2</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>54.4</td><td>52.2</td><td>54.1</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>48.5</td><td>45.9</td><td>47.1</td></tr><tr><td>Prices</td><td>53.4</td><td>59.2</td><td>58.1</td></tr></table>

Both the ISM and PMI surveys revealed strong gains in service sector business activity last month, following earlier mixed messages in the main May manufacturing surveys. After a big move down in April, the May ISM services survey rebounded 4.4-pts to 53.8—its highest reading since June 2023. Business activity saw the largest jump on the month, surging 10.3-pts to an 18-month high of 61.2. Smaller increases were recorded for May in the new orders and employment components, up a still solid 1.9 and 1.2-pts, respectively. That said, the employment index remains depressed at 47.1 and appears to have been on a downward trend for the past year. The supplier deliveries index also rebounded sharply in May to 52.7, which is the strongest reading since late 2022 and suggests longer delivery lags and potential renewed supply chain issues. However, this series has been quite volatile of late. The services ISM prices index was down marginally in May and has been volatile around a value similar to its pre-pandemic level. Note that the strength in services lifted the ISM (all-industry) composite 3.8-pts to 53.2, despite the modest pullback in the May manufacturing survey.

## International trade (Jun 6)

<table><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td colspan="2">Feb</td><td colspan="2">Mar</td><td rowspan="5">Apr</td><td rowspan="5"></td></tr><tr><td>Balance(BOP basis)</td><td>-69.5 -69.0</td><td></td><td>-69.4</td><td>-68.6</td><td>-77.0</td><td>-74.6</td></tr><tr><td>Services</td><td></td><td>22.2 24.2</td><td>23.1</td><td>24.8</td><td>22.7</td><td>24.6</td></tr><tr><td>Merchandise</td><td>-91.7 -93.2</td><td></td><td>-92.5</td><td>-93.3</td><td>99.7</td><td>-99.2</td></tr><tr><td>Exports (%m/m)</td><td>-2.2</td><td>2.7</td><td>-2.0</td><td>-1.7</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.8</td></tr><tr><td>Imports (%m/m)</td><td>2.3</td><td>2.7</td><td>-1.6</td><td>-1.5</td><td>27</td><td>2.4</td></tr></table>

The April trade balance widened significantly from March but slightly less than expected at -\$74.6bn. This deficit is notably wider than March<sup>’</sup>s revised -\$68.6bn trade balance (from -\$69.4bn previously). While the deficit came in a bit narrower than expected, April is still quite early in tracking “hard<sup>”</sup> data for current quarter growth. We still see net trade as likely to subtract nearly a full percentage point from GDP growth in 2Q. Meanwhile, revisions to prior months that accompanied this report suggest a slightly larger drag from net trade for 1Q GDP growth; all else equal they would imply a 1.2% q/q saar growth rate last quarter, down from the recently revised 1.3% pace and 1.6% as originally released.

Within the details of the April trade report, nominal goods and services exports rose 0.8% last month while nominal imports increased 2.4%; real growth rates were similar on the month for both merchandise exports and imports. The firmness in imports was driven in part by strong growth in capital goods; there also was a big rebound in autos imports whereas imports of consumer goods eased slightly in April. Note that the relative strength in imports, while a drag on headline GDP growth, does suggest demand remains resilient in the business capex space.

## Productivity and costs (Jun 6)

<table><tr><td>Nonfarm business sector, %q/q, saar, unless noted</td><td>Rev</td><td>Pre</td><td>Rev 1Q24</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Productivity</td><td>4Q23 3.5</td><td>1Q24 0.3</td><td></td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.9</td></tr><tr><td>Output</td><td>3.8</td><td>1.3</td><td>0.9</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Hourly compensation</td><td>06</td><td>0.6 5.0</td><td>64</td><td>4.2</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>44</td><td>4.4 4.7</td><td>40</td><td>3.8</td></tr><tr><td>Unit labor costs</td><td>40</td><td>-2.8 4.7</td><td>63</td><td>4.0</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>13</td><td>1.7 1.8</td><td>书</td><td>0.9</td></tr><tr><td>Hours</td><td>0.2</td><td>1.0</td><td>书</td><td>0.6</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.3</td><td>03</td><td>0.2</td></tr></table>

1Q labor productivity in the nonfarm business sector eased slightly to 0.2% q/q saar from 0.3% previously, while 1Q unit labor costs were revised down more meaningfully, to 4.0% q/ q saar from a prior 4.7%. Both beat expectations in that productivity growth was slightly stronger while ULC grew less than anticipated. While Fed officials should welcome any slowing in elevated ULC growth, they likely are not taking a particularly strong signal from this notoriously volatile series. On a four-quarter basis, 1Q ULC growth was revised down to 0.9%oya from 1.8% previously. Outside of the distortions during the early days of the pandemic, that is the slowest annual pace of increase in unit labor costs since late 2019.

However, Fed officials would prefer to see improving productivity helping to mute ULCs, whereas the revisions point instead to weaker than expected growth in compensation per hour as the main factor. Compensation growth remains firm at 4.2% annualized for the quarter and 3.8% over the past year—which had supported solid consumer spending—but is now showing signs of decelerating from the headier paces seen in recent prior quarters.

A downward revision to productivity was anticipated given the earlier announced revision lower to 1Q GDP growth, but growth in hours worked in the nonfarm business sector was also unexpectedly revised down last quarter. That said, yearago productivity growth remained unchanged at 2.9%oya; that pace reflects much higher quarterly growth rates for most of last year as strength on the supply side of the economy helped boost growth and contributed to the ongoing disinflation.
