rate (%)</td><td>3.8</td><td></td><td>3.9</td><td></td><td>39</td><td>4.0</td></tr><tr><td>Participation rate (%)</td><td>62.7</td><td></td><td>62.7</td><td></td><td></td><td>62.5</td></tr></table>

Job growth rebounded smartly in May, with nonfarm employment increasing 272,000 (prior months revised down 15,000 on net). Pay growth also picked back up as average hourly earnings increased 0.4%, taking the year-ago increase to 4.1%. The average workweek was unchanged at 34.3 hours. Meanwhile, the unemployment rate ticked up to 4.0%, the highest since January 2022, and most other metrics from the household survey were less favorable or outright weak. The household measure of employment contracted by 408,000, unemployment increased 157,000, and the participation rate slipped two ticks to 62.5%. Over the past 12 months the unemployment rate is up 0.4%-point, and during the same period nonfarm employment growth averaged 230,000 jobs per month. The confluence of these two facts points to (i) the importance of immigration in boosting trend, or breakeven, job growth, and (ii) the fact that the monthly establishment survey is likely overstating the pace of job growth.

After the May FOMC meeting, when the rate was 3.8%, Chair Powell said “a couple of tenths in the unemployment rate<sup>”</sup> would not be sufficient to ease, as he would need to see “broader<sup>”</sup> weakening. Last month<sup>’</sup>s perky job growth and persistent strength in wage growth are still very far from meeting that broader test, and a July ease now looks highly unlikely. It<sup>’</sup>s possible that the weakness in the household survey broadens out in the three employment reports between now and the September FOMC, but given the recent momentum in job growth, we think it may take the five reports available between now and the November FOMC meeting to meet the test. We now see the first ease in November, followed by a quarterly cadence of rate cuts next year.

In the details of the establishment survey, job growth was led again by health care, which added 84,000 jobs in May. After cooling in April, government employment increased 43,000 last month. While these industries led the way, job growth was broad based last month with 63.4% of industries increasing employment, the strongest reading on the diffusion index in over a year. The average workweek was unchanged near the low end of its recent range at 34.3 hours (34.296 hours to higher precision). The strength in headline earnings was mirrored in the production and non-supervisory worker series, where average hourly earnings increased 0.5% last month and the year-ago gain ticked up to 4.2%.

Turning to the household survey, some of last month<sup>’</sup>s increase in the unemployment rate was due to higher teen unemployment, though the 20+ rate also increased a tick to 3.6%, now up from a cycle low of 3.2%. The 376,000 yearago increase in household employment continues to contrast markedly with the 2.786 million increase reported in the establishment survey. Moreover, the payroll concept adjusted measure looks even weaker, down 456,000 last month and up only 195,000 over the last year. Mirroring the quits number in the JOLTS report, job leavers as a share of the unemployed continues to decline to a near three-year low. In contrast to the headline participation rate number, prime-age (25-54) participation ticked up last month to a cycle high 83.6%, and prime-age female participation hit an all-time high of 78.1%. The gains were offset by big declines in over 55 and under 25 participation rates.

## Focus: Expect downward revisions to (lagged) payrolls

Employment growth has shown some divergence between the household and establishment-based measures lately, and we have argued in the past that several factors might be at play. More recently, another important divergence attracted some attention, a widening gap among different surveys of establishments (Figure 1). There are two more sources of net employment change in addition to the BLS<sup>’</sup>s official payroll measure from the CES; employment growth computed from the Business Employment Dynamics (BED) and the one implied by the Quarterly Census of Employment and Wages (QCEW). All of these sources survey US establishments with different scope and frequency, and QCEW and BED lag the official payroll numbers from the CES by several months. Hence, one should not expect them to provide the exact same estimate for employment gains over time. Even so, the current level of divergence we have for 2023 between these surveys is likely suggesting a downward revision to payroll gains after the annual benchmark revision. Our analysis implies that the March 2024 level of employment in the CES might be eventually revised down by around 500,000. This would be equivalent to a reduction in monthly job growth of about 40,000 per month spread over a year, between March 2023 and March 2024, reducing monthly gains to a still solid pace of around 200,000 per month. Our estimates have uncertainty around them, and a similar analysis last year overestimated the magnitude of the eventual downward revision. One source of uncertainty is the potential revision we will get for December 2023 QCEW data, which will be available later.

Figure 1: Employment growth - Establishment surveys since 2022 1.5Ch oya, Millions - 2Q24 for CES equals the average of April and Ma
![](images/6e721315623312717b1e44adee866a53c5640e5640d3bb2e1314c7ef6671eae3.jpg)
The Quarterly Census of Employment and Wages (QCEW) serves as source data for the annual benchmark revisions to the monthly BLS data on nonfarm employment. The QCEW data lag the monthly payroll figures by about two quarters and are currently reported through December 2023. As the December 2023 data currently stand, the QCEW data show employment growth of 1.52% oya through that period, while the BLS<sup>’</sup>s establishment survey shows an increase of about

1.97% oya, implying about 670,000 less employment gains relative to a year ago by the former source (Figure 1). The estimate from BED, which lags QCEW by a month or so, was even weaker as of 3Q23.

Figure 2: Nonfarm employment benchmark revisions Revision to CES March employment level for specified benchmark year (%)
![](images/323da13e807f6379b8100c28fcd158e1ef2087a523211ae06ea61ffffe5150cd.jpg)

The BLS will release its annual preliminary benchmark announcement associated with establishment survey nonfarm employment on August 21. The announced revision will be associated with the March 2024 level of nonfarm employment and will be fully incorporated into the regular monthly BLS data early next year (when the January 2025 data are published in February). Until we receive the report on the preliminary benchmark in August, we can still exploit the availability of three-quarters of the relevant QCEW data through December 2023. Using the historical linear relationship that exists between the CES-QCEW gap observed at the end of the reference year and the eventual benchmark revision (Figure 2), we can obtain a reasonable estimate for the anticipated benchmark revision. Based on this relationship, the gap between the employment measures through December 2023 suggests that nonfarm employment for March 2024 will be revised down by about 0.3%.

Figure 3: Employment gains across establishment surveys over time Ch oya, Millions
![](images/75b2c08a045439cc0da6029e423c5d78a9e810882cf3cbb75243ed69ac475419.jpg)
It is important to note, however, that benchmark revisions do not necessarily cause these gaps to fully disappear (Figure 3). Historically, over a year ago employment gains implied by all these establishment surveys moved closely, but periods of significant gaps remain after many years, highlighting the underlying differences in scope.

## Euro Area

• ECB cuts rates and says that rates are still restrictive

• This keeps prospect of further rate cuts in place, but with more data-dependence

• Euro area consumers remain cautious despite strong real income gains

• German industry data a touch better

Having very clearly signalled a 25bp cut, the ECB delivered that move this week. In terms of the forward guidance, the direction of travel is still downwards. First, the ECB assesses the new deposit rate level of 3.75% as restrictive and Lagarde described it as “far away<sup>”</sup> from the neutral rate. Second, the new staff forecasts, which factor in further rate cuts, still show core inflation reaching 2%. For both reasons, Lagarde said during the press conference that there is a “strong likelihood<sup>”</sup> that the ECB has entered the “dialling back<sup>”</sup> phase of the restrictive stance. However, there was greater emphasis that this direction of travel has to be validated by the data.

In some sense, July is “live<sup>”</sup> for the next cut, but it is very likely from the pre-meeting commentary and also from Lagarde<sup>’</sup>s comments today that a cut in September is much more likely as there will be new staff projections to take stock of the outlook. We therefore stick with our call that the ECB will cut again in September and December. We also stick with our call for five additional cuts next year. Lagarde said today that the central bank is prepared for “bumps<sup>”</sup> in the disinflation journey, but the data will have to validate the inflation forecast in a broad sense and provide reassurance on some of the recent challenges. In this sense, visibility remains low, especially for next year.

Much will come down to how the data evolve. The ECB noted that domestic inflation and wage growth were still “elevated<sup>”</sup>. That was also reflected in the staff forecasts, which revised core inflation higher not just for the near-term but also for 2025. In fact, core inflation reaches 2% only in 2Q26 rather than in 3Q25 (Figure 1). Given that the downward revision of this in March had a seemingly large impact on the ECB<sup>’</sup>s thinking, prompting a strong signal of a June cut, it is remarkable that the reversal of this had little impact on this week<sup>’</sup>s decision. In fact, only one Governing Council member, reportedly Holzmann, dissented against the cut. Clearly, the ECB has become focused on the direction of travel, which is still towards 2%. But, the core inflation revision highlights a key challenge and that there is pressure for the incoming data to provide some reassurance. All of this will shape the policy outlook in the coming months.

Figure 1: Euro area core inflation
%oya, dotted line is ECB target for headline inflation
![](images/c7b4d38dda8e66cc7e2381a4193315e822bcec5c920ce028ecce51a72948ace7.jpg)

## Euro area consumers remain cautious

The monthly retail sales data are one of the more visible and timely indicators of Euro area consumer spending. Last year, retail sales declined at a 0.7%ar and data so far this year are mostly signalling stabilization. In April, retail sales fell 0.5%m/m, which is 0.4%ar below the 1Q24 average. However, consumer spending was already rising at a 0.7%ar last year and this pace was maintained in 1Q24 according to this week<sup>’</sup>s final GDP report (Figure 2). The breakdown into goods and services spending is somewhat partial as not all countries report these data, but hints at a modest decline in services. This could imply that some goods spending that is not covered by retail sales increased, but none of this is very clear.

Figure 2: Euro area consumer spending
![](images/8d3c73f00bf443f6829a451cb02b198af04719236d1244d9bfbfa0352b7c8a4a.jpg)

What is clearer is that consumer spending remains weak relative to the likely increase in real disposable income. Data on the latter is not yet available, but compensation of employees rose strongly in 1Q24 by 7.9%q/q saar. This reflected a 6.6%q/q saar gain in compensation per employee and a 1.3%q/q saar gain in employment. Compensation was similarly strong on a per hour basis. One caveat here is that the country aggregation was a bit weaker on employee compensation than the figure reported for the Euro area, but in any case compensation grew much more firmly than the 2.2%q/q saar increase in the consumption deflator (Figure 3). It is possible that disposable income grew less strongly than labor income, but these data are not yet available and unlikely prevent some further increase in the saving rate. Overall, this still makes consumers look somewhat cautious. But, as real income gains are likely to remain solid for the next few quarters and confidence has been gradually improving, a more pronounced increase in consumer spending remains likely.

Figure 3: Euro area households spending and income %q/q saar, 2qma
![](images/188747d37c26779eeab7ad3cedc6981d64da37d23b5d6f6f8aef84e4fb33f9a6.jpg)

## Germany industry: modestly positive

![](images/2afe87f2dc889ebfa06d11ec97584e743ccaca1c4b95bfa62cd0b2088acf2420.jpg)

The German manufacturing PMI has improved noticeably in recent months, but the starting point for that improvement was so low that the survey is still pointing to some contraction in activity in orders. Incoming industry data are broadly consistent with this but are also running ahead of that improvement in some cases. German IP is a case in point: the 0.3%m/m increase in manufacturing output in April left its level 2.4%ar above the 1Q24 average (Figure 4). The VDA data suggest that car production declined again in May, possibly reflecting calendar-adjustment difficulties over the April/ May period. This reinforces our sense that the PMI will have to increase further to validate a more persistent return to positive output growth. That also applies to the signal for the GVA data in manufacturing, which look much better than IP in a level sense, but also lost momentum last year.

tion, the export data are delivering a clearer improvement with gains in both real exports and imports (Figure 5). The former seems qualitatively consistent with the relative resilience in manufacturing export orders, while the increase in real imports appears encouraging about domestic demand.

In other industry reports, the trend in manufacturing orders is still weak, but April did see an encouraging increase in exbulk orders of 2.9%m/m. The latter only reverse the declines seen in 1Q24 and still leaves the trend looking modestly downward, but it was an improvement nonetheless. In addi-

Figure 5: German export and import volumes 4Q19 = 100
![](images/b427e390ae75c43b0598f72d3406aa64ef7a9c784ac8e383b61e76f15f8ddcbb.jpg)

## PMI pickup broadens

The final Euro area composite PMI was revised down 0.1pt to 52.2 in May, which is however still consistent with a 1.5%ar on GDP growth and up 0.5pt from April. In the detail, only the future output index was revised higher by 0.9pt, which is constructive, while other details were revised lower. At the country level, new information showed Italy (-0.3pt to 52.3) and Spain (+0.8pt to 56.6) still performing well, although the GDP implications are much stronger in Spain than in Italy (Figure 6). With Germany also at 52.4 now, the country divergences have narrowed with only France still standing out on the downside. At the sector level, manufacturing continues to lag services by a substantial margin and it is still pointing to contraction, but it has also improved recently, including in Germany.

Some countries already have released April IP data, including Germany (0.3%m/m), France (0.6%m/m) and Spain (0.0%m/ m) among the main economies of the region. Meanwhile, IP fell 2.5pts in Portugal. Using this information, we pencil in a 0.2%m/m increase in Euro area IP, which would leave IP on a trend up. The manufacturing PMI data have increased significantly of late but remain low by historical standards. While the signal of the survey has not been accurate in recent quarters, the survey still hints at weakness in manufacturing.

The German HICP data surprised us to the upside at the time of the flash release. No details will be published next week but we suspect that the early timing of Easter this year lifted services prices in May, explaining some of the surprise relative to our forecast. The CPI data can shed light on the holiday-related price dynamics, including airfares, package holiday prices and accommodation services.

German industrial orders fell 0.2%m/m in April but rose by a strong 2.9%m/m on an ex-bulk basis. The latter is only reversing the declines in 1Q24 and leaves the trend still modestly downward, which is consistent with the German manufacturing PMI. But, the extent of the weakness is easing and the PMI is clawing its way towards a stable level. In the orders data, exports orders have now been stable for almost a year, with a downward trend coming entirely from domestic orders.

<table><tr><td>2nd</td></tr><tr><td>Final Est. 4Q23 1Q24 1Q24</td></tr><tr><td>Euro area (final)</td></tr><tr><td>%q/q, sa -0.1 0.3 0.3 %q/q, saar -0.2 1.3 1.3</td></tr><tr><td>%oya 0.1 0.2 0.4 0.4</td></tr><tr><td>GDP components (%q/q saar)</td></tr><tr><td>Private consumption 0.2 0.7 书 0.7</td></tr><tr><td>Government consumption 21 2.3 15 0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Fixed investment 4.0 3.4 b -5.9</td></tr><tr><td>Exports Q0 0.8 甲 5.6</td></tr><tr><td>Imports 2.6 2.3 25 -1.3</td></tr><tr><td>Contribution to GDP growth (%q/q saar)</td></tr><tr><td>Domestic final sales 14 1.6 」5 -0.9</td></tr><tr><td>-04 -1.2 -1.3</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories 5 Net trade 1.2-0.6 12 3.5</td></tr></table>

Euro area GDP growth was left unrevised at 1.3%q/q saar in 1Q24. New details were weak at first sight in terms of domestic demand. But Ireland, which can be volatile, largely impacted these numbers (see tables below). Excluding Ireland, domestic final sales added 0.9%-pt to growth, with household spending up 0.6% ar and fixed investment up 2.3% ar (-5.9% ar including Ireland). Country releases already had hinted at a modest household spending gain (broadly on par with last year<sup>’</sup>s gains), which contrasts with stronger real income gains following the decline in inflation. We have noted previously that consumer confidence has increased but still remains below its historical average. Strong real income gains, in our view, should further boost sentiment and in return household consumption. Meanwhile, inventory changes were a 0.5%-pt drag on growth and net trade boosted growth by 0.8%-pt (ex. Ireland in both cases).

On the production side, the data continued to show that the services sector remains the main growth engine in the region. Private services were up 2.2% ar. Construction also rebounded in 1Q (2.5% ar), helped by better weather conditions, but manufacturing remains weak, with the Euro area print down 1.7% ar. Some of the weakness is linked to Ireland and the ex. Ireland print looks better at +0.7% ar, echoing PMI increases seen in recent months.

Overall, the news on the German labour market is solid, but with some details on the softer side. In May, the unemployment rate was stable at 5.9%, but the number of unemployed rose by 25,000 and vacancies fell by 5,000. However, these softer signs came alongside a relatively solid increase in employment in April of 25,000. Similarly, business surveys look decent. The composite PMI employment index has picked up, consistent with the pickup in employment. Only a weakening in the IAB labour market barometer tempers this signal somewhat. We note also that usage of the short-time work subsidy scheme has nudged higher recently on a fulltime basis. But, overall, the labour market still looks solid in level terms and increase in some of the employment indicators in the business surveys (PMI and IFO) suggests that some clearer improvement could lie ahead.

Euro area retail sales declined 0.5%m/m in April, reversing most of the 0.7%m/m gain in March. The April level is 0.4%ar below 1Q24, leaving the trend from the turn of the year broadly flat. While this looks disappointing, actual consumer spending has performed better than retail sales over the past year. Last year, retail sales contracted at a 0.7%ar while total consumer spending grew at a 0.6%ar.

## Japan

• BoJ to signal balance sheet policy shift at next week<sup>’</sup>s meeting

• Focus on pace of reduction of JGB purchases...

• ...and on BoJ<sup>’</sup>s assessment of necessary conditions for upcoming rate hikes

• Incoming data continue to suggest higher wages, tentative recovery in demand

Several media reports this week have suggested that the BoJ is preparing to formalize a change in balance sheet policy at next week<sup>’</sup>s policy meeting. This is in line with our longstanding forecast for a shift in June, and we expect the BoJ to provide further clarity on its plans for a reduction in JGB purchases, i.e., balance sheet rundown, next week (Figure 1). While we do not expect a shift to an outright autopilot-style step down in buying, we do think the BoJ will want to signal that further cuts in purchases will be gradual, predictable, and largely mechanical—such that the market reemerges as the key price-setter for yields.

Figure 1: BoJ balance sheet, selected asset purchases JPY tn, rolling net 12-week sum, both scales
![](images/1bd1bbd339a78781052804edb459d12c7e520fb8ef302dbd5f97f6238d5320be.jpg)
Source: BoJ, J.P. Morgan

Underlying this view is the idea that BoJ will no longer lean on its balance sheet as a monetary policy tool, thereby leaving the policy rate as the key determinant of its policy bias. Against this backdrop, we do not expect an explicit signal on the timing of the next rate hike to emerge next week. But we expect Ueda to provide further clarity on the necessary conditions for the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s next policy rate increase, which we continue to pencil in for July.

Away from policy, the past week<sup>’</sup>s data were in line with our forecasts. The MoF<sup>’</sup>s 1Q corporate survey suggested firms spending on capex softened in 1Q after an end-2023 surge, and we see the risk of a minor downward revision to the second estimate of 1Q GDP released next week. But against the backdrop of near record-high economy-wide profit margins and persistent labor supply tightness, we expect momentum in corporate investment to gain pace through 2H24. Indeed, wage data for April suggested that firms are facing continued pressure to hike pay. Growth in scheduled earnings hit a fresh over 20-year high, and assuming that the strong Shunto negotiation outcomes continue to filter into average pay growth, we expect real wages will also turn up through midyear.

## BoJ to signal balance sheet reduction

We have long argued that the BoJ has strong incentive to start a balance sheet reduction early, ahead of rate hikes, particularly due to its bloated size. And three months after the NIRP removal, the BoJ likely will make the decision next week to reduce its pace of JGB purchases from the current level, which is currently broadly consistent with keeping the balance sheet size unchanged. Governor Ueda has already expressed a policy direction to reduce the balance sheet sometime soon, echoed by recent signals from other Policy Board members. We think chances for the BoJ to formalize the policy at the upcoming meeting are quite high.

That said, the key focus on the decision is how the BoJ will announce the schedule of future JGB purchases. The BoJ has already made it clear that it does not use the balance sheet as a monetary policy tool, and intends to gradually restore the market's price-setting function in the JGB market. However, the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s greater discretion under the current JGB purchase program (about JPY6 trillion per month with +/-1 trillion discretion) creates a situation where the market continues to focus on BoJ purchases. To avoid this, the BoJ appears to prefer to conduct future JGB purchases in a somewhat predictable manner. The BoJ may set the QT process with less discretion (though not fully moving to autopilot), to further restore market pricing mechanism.

The distance to additional rate hikes will also be an important focus. We think the BoJ is unlikely to hike the policy rate together with the decision on a balance sheet reduction, as the BoJ may want to avoid a sharp shift in expectations that would lead a surge in JGB yields. And we continue to believe that the next rate hike will come in July. Despite sluggish consumption demand, Japan<sup>’</sup>s inflation is expected to jump after May CPI due to the expiration of energy subsidies. Wages are on the rise as a result of the spring wage negotiations, and a fixed-amount tax reduction will also be paid in June.

Under these circumstances, if slow BoJ policy adjustment keeps the yen under constant pressure, it could exacerbate negative sentiment among domestic consumers through rising inflation expectations. Balancing these risks, we continue to expect the BoJ to raise the policy rate to 0.25% in July. Although we don<sup>’</sup>t expect the BoJ to send a clear message on the timing of the next rate hike, how Ueda describes the preconditions for additional rate hikes at the post-meeting press conferences likely will provide hints about the future rate path.

## Fresh highs for April wages

Headline wages rose 2.1%oya in April, a touch stronger than our forecast and higher than consensus expectations, both of which were for a 1.8% rise. Underlying details look strong. Scheduled earnings, which exclude volatile special payments, rose to 2.3%oya, marking a fresh two-decade high, led by a sustained trend higher in full-time pay. Constant sample data (which removing sampling volatility) suggested wage growth moderated slightly to 1.7%oya from 1.9% in March (Figures 2 and 3).

Figure 2: Japan total cash earnings
![](images/31290ac00dd97bf2f8d0e12d83e8135b8ae683b4ee42c4ba4630e03756ea3495.jpg)

Figure 3: Japan scheduled earnings
![](images/1ff26d003f5890c85e472361130458efa27bc75b41e6093fdb8163877b71c1bd.jpg)

Figure 4: Japan total and scheduled earnings, real
![](images/2760f3d7dbc3e0f5e1bd94ed74b2f582955ccfd1320252a7e1195d7f4dcc3182.jpg)

Looking through the noise in the monthly wage data, the trend continues to be for higher underlying base pay levels. Strong outcomes from the initial rounds of the Shunto negotiations should filter into the monthly wage data over the next couple of months, pushing average wage growth higher still.

Assuming that base pay hikes agreed in the negotiations end at the mid-3% mark, we think economy-wide full-time earnings growth is likely to trend to 2.5%-3%oya this year. This should be sufficient to pull average real wage growth into positive territory through 2H24.

For context, real wage growth in Japan over the past twenty years has averaged -0.7%oya, and the over-year-ago rate has been negative for just over 60% of that period. Real wages have fallen at a sharper pace than the long-run average over the prior six months, averaging -2%, but falling at their shallowest pace since end-2022 in April, matching the long-run average (Figure 4). If we are correct in judging that nominal wage growth has further to run, real income drags should continue to fade, supporting domestic activity.

## Profits rise, capex softens in MoF survey

The Ministry of Finance<sup>’</sup>s corporate survey for 1Q24 came broadly in line with our expectations, with a 6.8%oya rise in nominal capex (JPM: 7%; consensus: 11%). This translates into a 2%q/q saar sequential fall in nominal capex, on the heels of an almost 37% surge in 4Q23. By sector, manufacturers<sup>’</sup> capital expenditure fell 11.3%q/q saar, while outlays by non-manufacturers rose 4%.

Other details of the survey suggested a still-robust trend in corporate activity. Economy-wide sales were down 1.5%q/q saar, their first quarterly pullback since 1Q21. But weakness was concentrated among manufacturers, potentially reflecting supply-chain constraints that had depressed the manufacturing sector in early 2024. On the flipside, non-manufacturers reported a 0.9%q/q saar rise in sales, and a near-34% surge in current profits. Taking a step back, economy-wide profit margins continue to trend higher, and are at (or nearly at) their all-time highs across sectors (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Japan Economy-wide corporates' ordinary profit margin % of sales, sa
![](images/dd555980d32ed503807f7c94291f91ea536be9281a95ca51602114aad18b5e6e.jpg)

We think the capex data in the MoF survey will lead to a downward revision to real business investment in the second estimate of 1Q24 GDP (Figure 6). If realized, this also suggests a minor downward revision to headline GDP last quarter. We do not think soft growth over the past several months will form a key input into the BoJ policy trajectory at this point, however.

Figure 6: Japan Nominal capex %q/q, saar

![](images/6d125b06c837a5346d713401c4c50bff18db117bc840dd6a88ac6dbc03aa6f62.jpg)
Source: MoF, Cabinet Office

## Canada

The Bank of Canada cut the policy rate 25bp this week as widely expected

The Bank signaled that it’s reasonable to expect further policy rate cuts...

• ...but interest rate decisions will be taken one meeting at a time

The Bank of Canada eased monetary policy this week, cutting the policy rate 25bp to 4.75% as we expected. The Bank will continue its policy of QT. The announcement noted that “with continued evidence that underlying inflation is easing, Governing Council agreed that monetary policy no longer needs to be as restrictive.<sup>”</sup> Recent data have increased Bank confidence that inflation will continue to move toward the 2% target. The Bank stated that risks to the inflation outlook remain, however, and the same factors that could drive inflation pressures higher must continue to be monitored: inflation expectations, wage growth, and corporate pricing behavior. We main tain our view that the Bank will lean toward a cautious and gradual approach to easing in 2H24. We expect the next policy rate cut will be in 4Q24, but based on the evolution of inflation so far in 2024 the risks are that the next policy rate cut comes sooner than we are expecting.

Governor Macklem<sup>’</sup>s opening statement at the press conference elaborated on the monetary policy decision and sounded a bit more dovish. It was noted that the indicators of underlying inflation increasingly point to a pattern of sustained easing, and “it is reasonable to expect further cuts to our policy interest rate.<sup>”</sup> The statement highlighted the substantial progress on the evolution of inflation. The three-month run rates of the Bank<sup>’</sup>s core measures have slowed from 3.5% in December to under 2% in March and April, and the easing in inflation has been broad based with a declining share of prices above the Bank<sup>’</sup>s target range (Figure 1). But Governor Macklem noted that interest rate decisions would be taken one meeting at a time, and the Bank doesn<sup>’</sup>t plan to jeopardize the progress already made on fighting inflation.

Figure 1: Breadth of elevated inflation
![](images/783d81dcff8d2ba6bd2d8ec874bf0404fd0c8d4a1d2fb6ef4ba0ab4f345631b6.jpg)

Governor Macklem took a couple questions in the press conference on how much Bank of Canada monetary policy can diverge from Federal Reserve monetary policy. He noted that there are limits to how much the policy rates can diverge but said “we<sup>’</sup>re not close to that limit.<sup>”</sup> He mentioned that the historical record presents a guideline for the divergence. Since the early 2000s the widest negative divergence (BOC policy rate below Fed Funds rate) has been 100bps.

## Cool to the touch

Labor Force Survey employment was up 26,700 in May, more favorable than we expected. The unemployment rate was 6.2% in May, up a tick compared with April and the highest rate since January 2022. Job gains were led by the services sector (47,400) with a partial offset by the goods sector (-20,700), generally the pattern seen so far in 2024. And the gain was all in part-time work; full-time employment declined by 36,000 in May. The employment rate—the proportion of the population aged 15 and older who are employed—declined 0.1%pts to 61.3% in May, the seventh decrease in the past eight months. The May employment report continued to paint a picture of a cooling labor market into the summer—except for wage growth. The annual pace of average hourly earnings for permanent employees rebounded to 5.2%oya in May following growth of 4.8%oya in April (nsa). The three-month run rate bounced up from 3.0%,saar in April to 5.4%,saar in May (our seasonal adjustment). That said, the trend is still well off the 9.0%,saar pace of September 2023.

Figure 2: Part time for economic reasons
![](images/0d96ab00cf78abd3af3a1f9580e8d176e10e2f62167490f0c22022fabed9b73c.jpg)

The May report included a Statistics Canada carve-out discussing the sharp up-trend in involuntary part-time workers (Figure 2). The involuntary part-time rate was 18.2% in May, up from 15.4% 12 months ago. Notable increases have been seen in the rate of prime age women reporting involuntary part-time (22.6%,up 2.9%pts compared with last year) and among young men aged 15 to 24 (17.7%, up 5.6%pts compared with last year). The number of involuntary part-time workers in May was the highest since March 2021 (our seasonal adjustment).

## Mexico

Morena wins big in the federal elections and is well positioned to dominate the legislative agenda

May inflation on the benign front, but given our updated Fed forecasts and institutional risks ...

… we do not expect a cut in June anymore; rates at 10.50% this year, followed by 9% in 2025

• Next week<sup>’</sup>s April IP to confirm our expectations for a swift rebound in 2Q24

Last weekend<sup>’</sup>s election delivered a landslide victory for the incumbent administration, which managed not only to secure the continuity of ruling-party Morena in the presidency by a substantial margin but also looks set to provide the next administration with a near-constitutional majority in both chambers of congress.

According to preliminary results (95% of votes counted), incumbent party Morena and its allies look set to secure an overwhelming majority of 70% in the lower house and of roughly 63% in the upper chamber (Figure 1). This has raised questions about the potential reach of changes to Mexico<sup>’</sup>s legal framework as a 66% majority in both chambers, alongside a 51% majority in local congresses, are the thresholds for a government to be able to pursue changes to the constitution. This had only recently been achieved by the 2012-18 Peña Nieto (EPN) administration through a broad coalition of Mexico<sup>’</sup>s “traditional parties,<sup>”</sup> PRI, PAN, and PRD.

Figure 1: Legislative elections
![](images/d6e5b921d7e3a5023d0689ed44b1b808ee2713aec178ef3d18273138d687af57.jpg)
Source: INE. Preliminary results obtained from the INE's Fast-counting. Results represent interval mid-point

Under the past administration the lack of a qualified majority in congress kept the government from pursuing substantial changes to Mexico<sup>’</sup>s legal framework, but not because it lacked the willingness to do so. These changes spanned from reforms to the country<sup>’</sup>s regulation of the energy sector to other matters such as the structure of the judicial power as well as that of autonomous regulators, stoking concerns about an overreach from the executive power. With preliminary results in hand, such potential changes have come back to the fore-

front. Other potential changes comprise the elevation of social spending and minimum wage increments (in real terms) to a constitutional level, as well as changes to the current pension system. that will challenge the sustainability of public finances.

If the incoming administration, however, decides to implement a much-needed fiscal reform, the sheer composition of congress should pave the way for swift approval. Our base case scenario is a watered-down fiscal plan that will center on higher (existing) taxes such as higher income tax brackets and excise taxes. Incoming president Sheinbaum mentioned recently that she intends to focus on making the tax regime more efficient and on the digitalization of tax contributors.

## Never mind inflation, Banxico to delay cuts

With inflation delivering mostly good news in May, as core goods continued a swift downward adjustment and core services slowly moved south, rate cuts should be a straightforward decision for the central bank. In particular, the June decision for Banxico should<sup>’</sup>ve been straightforward considering the recent speeches from the board, where it was clear that the majority of members were in favor of reducing the tight policy stance, with the nominal policy rate at 11% and the real ex-ante at 7%.

As things stand right now, we find it very likely that the board will have to take another breather in the next couple of meetings and will have to extend the pause until September. With our US team already postponing the start of the gradual easing cycle from the Fed until November and the domestic political environment becoming more uncertain after the landslide win for Morena and the back-and-forth comments from its Congress whips, we do not think there are conditions for resuming the easing cycle for the time being.

We now expect only two 25bp cuts this year (Figure 2), in September and December, bringing the policy rate to 10.50%, with rates at 9% in 2025.

Figure 2: Projected spread between Banxico and the Fed %-pts
![](images/a20fc8e5445d50316716d6e11431f87da07bba32c2795a877f03d07b232cfd0c.jpg)

Coming back to this week<sup>’</sup>s inflation report, details proved, at first glance, better than expected, with a - 0.19%m/m print, well below our expectation and the market<sup>’</sup>s (-0.03%m/m and -0.06%, respectively). The negative print was not very surprising given the seasonal effect from the electricity subsidy, but the surprise came as agricultural prices came in much lower, driven by fresh fruit and vegetables. Looking deeper into the details, the same old story within the core component, up 0.17%m/m (Figure 3) continues. Goods came in at a welcoming 0.07%m/m even though services once again proved stickier at 0.3%m/m. Yet the print should be digested as good news, with the overall dynamics within core continuing to trend toward Banxico<sup>’</sup>s target and more in line with pre-pandemic dynamics now.

Figure 3: Stability of inflation components in May
![](images/0e9da28a3c4f13a3ad3110b51282c96436814b5cf73392d05ba317857fd5af09.jpg)

Yes, annualized data for inflation is quite encouraging, and even annualized services is at its lowest since Sept 2023, and that is why we are maintaining our view of additional cuts, but Banxico will need to play a balancing act given the current juncture. As things stand, inflation for 2Q is tracking Banxico<sup>’</sup>s forecasts, with headline inflation pointing to 4.6%oya and core at 4.2%.

But with MXN starting to turn more volatile and depreciating faster than we expected, the effect on core goods (Figure 3) inflation could be a thorn in Banxico<sup>’</sup>s side sooner rather than later – if such an adjustment in the market proves to be persistent. Our new call for Banxico, however, is not only rooted in the changing dynamics in local assets, but, more importantly, in the institutional risks embedded in the forthcoming constitutional changes planned for the next ordinary sessions of Congress. The first session starts in September.

Even if not all of the intended plans are accomplished, economic implications will reverberate with the terminal policy rate expected to be higher than before to account for an increase in the country risk premium. We expect the policy rate at 9% next year, with not much clarity for further cuts, and a lot will depend on the reach and depth of the fiscal changes this year and next.

## Brazil

• 1Q GDP expanded 3.1%q/q saar, above expectations

• Even with a deceleration to 1% in 2Q due to the floods, we still expect GDP to grow 2.2% this year

• Manufacturing production grew for the fifth consecutive month

• This solid growth should reinforce BCB<sup>’</sup>s caution

We have been arguing that Brazil was well positioned to grow this year, given the healthiness of private-sector balance sheets. In our view, 1Q GDP performance reinforced that case. Last quarter, GDP expanded by a stronger than expected 3.1%q/q saar, above our expectation for a 2.4% expansion, which was above the consensus call at the beginning of the year. We look for growth to be running above 2% on average for the remainder of the year (Figure 1).

%q/q saar, forecasts in orange

![](images/e70404c50ceb6f980759e1e72384235a84fd0511328290d7f2f002fbe61809fe.jpg)

Part of the 1Q strength may have been the result of the 1% of GDP one-off payment of court-ordered debt (precatórios), justifying the private consumption jump of 6%q/q saar after falling in 4Q, and the rise of almost 13%q/q saar in retail sales. But GDP seemed to be already expanding at a solid pace last year, even when discounting the effect from the agricultural sector. For example, investment jumped 17.5% in 1Q, recovering some of the losses last year.

One of the main challenges to this solid GDP growth is the disaster in the southernmost state of Brazil that should drag 2Q GDP. The extent of the damage to economic growth remains unclear. So far, we know that manufacturing production kept growing in April, before the disaster, continuing the four-quarter expansion seen up to 1Q24, and sentiment indicators are suggesting limited impact, but car sales and production were significantly hit in May (Figures 2 and 3). For now, assuming that the floods will drag GDP by about 0.2%-points in 2Q, we see the economy expanding a lackluster 1%q/q saar in 2Q. The nowcaster currently stands at 2.8%, but with little data released for May, when the floods became critical.

Figure 1: Quarterly real GDP growth
Figure 2: Manufacturing and services PMI
![](images/92b495020177f95096b82c209fdcaa3b1cf8481ed2223bf018d57fb9b72404d1.jpg)

Figure 3: Cars and light vehicles production and sales Thousands of vehicles, sa
![](images/3df484c7980aad16084c59456673fcac979ad01734bff3c3109507e2baa0f5a1.jpg)

Further ahead, several factors suggest that Brazil<sup>’</sup>s business cycle is still in its expansion phase. The absence of extended spending relative to private sector incomes is, in our view, reflected in the fact that the private savings rate has moved up in recent quarters, including 1Q. Leverage appears well below the pre-pandemic level in the banking sector, too, underpinning solid credit growth despite high real rates. On the business side, profit margins remained relatively high, despite falling since the pandemic peak, as revenue growth is underperforming the increase in costs, particularly labor costs recently (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Profit margin and job creation
![](images/8758b5b871e94dc15f1957e5f1ae2389c34da0cdcd9c31a1f74b38f200a865d7.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P. and J.P. Morgan

As the flood-related situation normalizes and reconstruction efforts pick up at the end of 2Q and beginning of 3Q, we expect GDP to rebound 3.6%q/q saar in 3Q . This path keeps our 2024 GDP estimate stable at 2.2%y/y. In this context, we see the Brazilian economy growing at a solid pace this year, with a more substantial deceleration coming only next year.

While there wasn<sup>’</sup>t any major surprise with economic activity this week, the resilience of the economy only reinforces our view that BCB will be cautious ahead. The GDP growth performance adds to the rise of medium-term inflation expectations that should make the COPOM pause the easing cycle at the next meeting, with the SELIC rate at 10.50%.

Ebbs and flows of politics will likely continue to make headlines until then. Despite the more challenging scenario for the revenue agenda, the government sent to Congress a proposal to limit the use of PIS/Cofins tax credits with immediate effect this week. The announcement follows several months of negotiation on payroll tax breaks and is an effort to offset its negative fiscal impacts – by increasing net revenues by BRL29bn vs the estimated loss of BRL26bn, according to the Finance Ministry. Several lawmakers criticized the measure, arguing that there are other alternatives that would be less disruptive for important sectors, such as agribusiness (<sup>‘</sup>Senadores criticam medida que compensa desoneração e pedem estudo de impacto,<sup>’</sup> Folha, June 6). If approved, the measure would likely lead to increased tax collection not only this year but also in 2025, helping bridge the BRL50bn gap between National Treasury<sup>’</sup>s forecast and the balanced target.

## Argentina

• We estimate May CPI at 5.1%m/m, driving the overyear-ago CPI print to 279.1%oya

We see year-end inflation at 155%oya amid still sizable regulated price increases in the pipeline

Real tax collection interrupted a series of eight consecutive negative prints in May, increasing by 11.1%oya

Securing higher income sources (fiscal package) remains paramount while waiting for activity to recover

• BCRA<sup>’</sup>s FX purchases at the official FX have slowed amid the widening of the FX gap

## CPI to decelerate, but core to prove sticky

We estimate May CPI at 5.1%m/m, driving the over-year-ago CPI print to 279.1%oya. Our headline inflation forecast (INDEC<sup>’</sup>s National CPI) is a bit above the 4.9%m/m suggested by the high-frequency data set tracked by EcoGo through May-end. The monthly inflation print would thus continue its deceleration path from 8.8%m/m in April, but different from prior months, in which core CPI spearheaded the monthly deceleration, last month the inflation slowdown was mainly explained by the postponement of regulated price increases and seasonal prices.

Regulated price increases in the pipeline will add upward pressure to headline inflation ahead. Our prior base case assumed the front-loading of the bulk of the tariff adjustments in 1H24, but the above noted postponements are to put upward pressure on headline inflation ahead. Indeed, the government announced that the increase in electricity and gas tariffs will resume in June, with increases of up to 156% on the former and up to 32% on the latter. This would add to hikes in transportation tariffs and fuels also in the pipeline. Against this backdrop, inflation would average 5.5%m/m in our estimates through the remainder of the year, consistent with yearend inflation at 155%oya. Advancing toward reducing the (still large) relative prices misalignment in a prompt manner and releasing capital controls are necessary conditions for a sustainable disinflation path ahead.

## Securing higher revenues is paramount

Real tax collection interrupted a sequence of eight consecutive negative prints in May, increasing by 11.1%oya. However, the monthly increase was not enough to offset the revenue pothole of the first months of the year, with the YTD tax collection still in the red at -5.3%oya. Note that the good performance of tax revenues on the month was mainly driven by record-high income tax collection from corporates (+81%oya), which saw year-end 2023 balances boosted by

the capital gains arising from FX devaluation. This was compounded by higher foreign trade–related taxes as well as by favorable calendar effects (two more business days relative to May <sup>’</sup>23).

Indeed, when excluding foreign trade–related, PAIS tax, and also the one-off increase of the income tax, the real contraction in tax revenues deepened in May, down by -19.7%oya (-14.3%, prior 3mma) and -13.9% YTD (Figure 1).

![](images/d3cc2d385f5409ae2fcb90547777489e1dce981d55e7585cefd8e3c52e073a08.jpg)

Despite May<sup>’</sup>s good performance, securing higher income sources remains paramount to consolidate the fiscal accounts ahead. The harvest should continue to support foreign trade– related tax collection in the next few months, but a rebound in sequential activity appears as a necessary condition for the sustainability of the fiscal consolidation over time. In the transition toward an economic activity rebound, securing additional income sources is also paramount.

Against this backdrop, after being approved in the Lower House and in the Senate commissions, we now watch for the Omnibus bill 2.0 and the fiscal package to be discussed in the Senate plenum next week (June 10). The voting outcome is expected to be tight, but our base case remains for the bills to be approved with changes. From a fiscal perspective, the most relevant changes involve a tax amnesty as well as the reincorporation of the income tax for the fourth category, jointly accounting for about 0.7% of GDP in new revenues in our estimates this year.

On the other side, the Lower House approved yesterday a new pension formula that, among other changes, includes an additional 7.2% increase in pensions to compensate for January<sup>’</sup>s real loss, carrying a fiscal cost of about 0.35% of GDP this year. While President Milei announced he will veto that bill once approved by the Senate, the eventual veto override by Congress (requiring two-thirds of the members present in each Chamber) would partially offset the revenue windfall from the Fiscal Package. We thus keep for now our forecast for a fiscal primary balance this year, yet we flag upside risks conditional on Omnibus bill 2.0 and fiscal package approval.

## Wider FX gap, slower reserve accumulation

The cash-basis current account posted a US\$2.3bn surplus in April, driving the YTD surplus to US\$7.6bn, coming from a US\$4.8bn deficit in the same period last year. The strong reversal was explained by the cash-basis merchandise trade balance improvement, US\$11bn above the same period last year. As in prior months, the gain was mostly explained by lower imports payments (down by US\$10.9bn compared with the same period last year), while exports have remained flat YTD (bear in mind that 20% of total exports are being supplied to the financial FX, as discussed below). Admittedly, the activity contraction has added downward pressure on imported quantities, but the plunge in cash imports has been also driven by the new import payment restrictions as the BCRA has only been allowing for the payment of imports in four installments, the flip-side of new commercial (imports) debt accumulation. Indeed, between December and April, new commercial debt has increased by US\$12.6bn. That compares to the central bank buying US\$14.7bn in the official FX market in the same period and to US\$8.4bn in Bopreal issued through April.

Regarding the international reserves accumulation pace, the fact that 20% of the export inflows are being supplied to the financial FX (around US\$6.2bn between December and April in our estimates) has limited the ability to accumulate reserves faster. Indeed, while exports inflows to the official FX have remained flat YTD, they have increased by 25% from the same period last year when taking into account the inflows also settled in the parallel FX market, thanks to the agri and energy sectors.

At the margin, however, the FX gap has widened, and the BCRA reserves accumulation pace has decelerated. Despite the sort of normalization in agri exports, the aggressive BCRA<sup>’</sup>s rate cuts over the past month and delays on the Omnibus bill 2.0 and fiscal package approval prompted an increase in the FX gap to around 45% by June 4. Against this backdrop, BCRA<sup>’</sup>s FX purchases at the official FX declined to US\$37mn per day in the last five-day average, from US\$167mn average in the month of April (Figure 2).

Ahead, monitoring the evolution of the exporters<sup>’</sup> supply, not only from the agri sector but also from the rest of the sectors, will be key to assess the limits of the current policy framework, particularly when taking into account that the exporters<sup>’</sup> real effective exchange rate has appreciated by 36% YTD and is also 13% below the 2023 average level.

Figure 2: Reserves accumulation and FX gap
![](images/3d110118deca71b07fb7835cd01d58b319cd6ab6bc8e94922f3eb5208ca6c61e.jpg)

We estimate net FX reserves of -US\$1.6bn as of May 23 (our estimate considers as hard-currency liabilities Bopreal series 2 amortizations for US\$1.9bn in the next 12 months but not Treasury deposits). Meanwhile, USD liquid reserves stood at \~US\$6.3bn (from almost null when the administration took office). Of note, the BCRA has to repay US\$4.9bn (US\$2.9bn in June and US\$2bn in July) that was tapped from the PBoC swap line last year. Negotiations for a rollover of at least a portion of it lingers. We insist that advancing toward the release of capital controls is paramount to secure and accelerate the ability to replenish reserves in the medium term.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

• Chile: April headline activity slipped by -\*0.3%m/m, sa, while ex. mining printed flat

• 2Q24 GDP revised lower to -0.9%ar, but activity is poised to accelerate in 3Q

• Colombia: The CAD compressed to 1.9% of GDP in 1Q24, which compares to 3.6% in 1Q23...

• … but net FDI started to decline from 2023<sup>’</sup>s highs, to 2.5% of GDP in 1Q24 (from 4.3% in 1Q23)

2024 CAD projected at 2.6% of GDP; the basic balance to return to negative territory, at -0.7% of GDP

## Chile: Ex. mining activity stays steady

The monthly headline activity index slipped by -0.3 %m/m, sa, in April, coming on the heels of the -0.6 % plunge recorded in March (revised from -0.7%, previously). When the last three-month sequential performance is analyzed, upon revisions, headline activity ran at 5.0%3m/3m saar, decelerating from the strong +7.5%ar logged in the first quarter of the year. In all, by April headline activity has gained a still remarkable +1.7%,sa, when compared to December<sup>’</sup>s level.

On an over-year-ago basis, activity recorded +3.5%oya in April. But it should be noted the favorable calendar effects, with three more labor days this year. Once adjusted for calendar effects, the growth rate logged +2.1%.

As usual, we focus on ex. mining activity, the most relevant indicator from a cyclical standpoint. Ex.mining printed flat on the month, coming on the heels of a -0.5% contraction in March (Figure 1). The sequential pace on a three-month basis decelerated to +2.8 %3m/3m saar, after posting in the first quarter of the year the strongest quarterly expansion pace since late 2021 (4.5 %ar).

Figure 1: Chile economic activity
![](images/4fe1513d72f72643da1f9c83ecbd7d1a8b6e6f20ed18ad5f9213a89fc50c76ab.jpg)

In all, despite the performance observed in the last two months, we maintain in broad terms the narrative for firmer growth than the prior year, a result of less monetary restriction compounded to still expansive fiscal policy and improved terms of trade impulse. After the stellar performance observed in the first quarter of the year (+7.8%ar), we input in our base case a strong deceleration in terms of sequential growth in 2Q24, which we now see as a sequential contraction for -0.9%ar. But momentum is poised to accelerate in 3Q (projected at +2.6%ar). The 2024 annual GDP projection is maintained at +2.7%y/y, upon revisions now consistent with +2.9%Q4/Q4.

## Colombia: The CAD narrowed in 1Q

The current account deficit logged US\$1.9bn in the first quarter of the year, equivalent to 1.9% of GDP. The current account thus continued the adjustment in place since mid 2022, with the deficit narrowing by 1.8%-pt of GDP compared to the 1Q23 (Figure 2). The quarterly deficit was explained by the income rents outflow, which totalled US\$3.3bn in the quarter, followed by the merchandise trade deficit, for US\$1.9bn. On the opposite side, transfers provided an inflow for+US\$3.3bn, up by 7.6%oya. When compared to the same quarter last year, the fall in both merchandise imports and exports stands out, as well as the gains for services exports.

Figure 2: Colombia current account balance US\$bn
![](images/4077175e62e7c72f67e034799c8665acbe5a4defc98c725207ab5b8974b15fc9.jpg)
Source: BanRep and J.P.Morgan

On the financial side, net FDI inflows reached US\$2.5bn (or 2.5% of GDP), down by US\$1.0bn compared to 1Q23. The basic balance (i.e., current account balance plus net DI flows), a gauge of the reliance on external portfolio inflows, therefore narrowed to a 0.6% of GDP surplus in 1Q24, which compares to 1.3% of GDP for the whole year 2023. Note the last time Colombia enjoyed a basic surplus of a similar magnitude on a five-quarter basis was in 2005-08.

Our base case is for growth to firm up in the second half of the year, which should drive imports higher, so for the current account deficit to print US\$10.7bn (2.6% of GDP). On FDI, we expect inflows to ease, particularly the ones related to oil and mining. Our baseline assumes net FDI logging US\$7.8bn for full-year 2024. The basic balance thus is projected to reverse into the red next year. Indeed, the basic balance (current account balance plus net FDi flows) is projected to print a -0.7% GDP deficit in 2024, reversing the 1.8% surplus recorded in 2023.

The negative net international investment position reached US\$191.1bn, or 49.4% of GDP by 1Q24. By the end of the period, assets amounted to US\$260.8bn (67.5% of GDP) and liabilities US\$451.8bn (116.9% of GDP). Of the total liabilities, note that 57.3% corresponds to foreign direct investment and only 20.6% to portfolio investments.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Review of past week’s data

## United Kingdom

• Election debate enters full swing

• Latest polls show large Reform Party gain

• DMP survey shows large drop in expected pay growth

This week brought the first of two planned TV debates ahead of the July election. Polling suggests a draw in terms of public perceptions, but arguably the more significant news of the week was Nigel Farage<sup>’</sup>s decision to stand as leader of the Reform Party. Initial polls might be capturing a knee-jerk move that is not sustained, but they show a rise in support for the Reform Party at the expense of Labour and the Conservatives (Figure 1). This could further divide the right-wing vote in particular, and potentially leave Labour in an even stronger position. There was limited data this week, although one of the surveys we pay more attention to - DMP wage expectations - showed a large drop that could help the BoE argue for an August cut (as we explain below). Next week the Labour market release will be scrutinised to see how much April wages increased following the minimum wage rise. Early indications from the payroll data suggest a potentially large impact on the month, and we continue to see August as a close call with risks tilted towards a later start to easing.

Figure 1: UK polls tracker for next general election
![](images/6745a440147abc3a5887aaf5c453ec6064e6dd96d4de6d082e85a36016c837aa.jpg)
Source: Polling companies, J.P. Morgan

## DMP shows pay growth drop

In May, the BoE placed significant weight on several survey measures in expressing higher confidence about future inflation and attempting a pivot to a June cut. June is now off the table following a huge gain in April services prices, and to a lesser extent the early election, but we were surprised by the BoE<sup>’</sup>s focus on some of the surveys given its mixed record in tracking the data and the still strong momentum in the wage and price data themselves. One survey that does seem to have worked a bit better since the pandemic, however, is the wage reading from the DMP business survey.

The latest DMP survey for May, released this week, showed a fall in year-ahead wage expectations from 4.6% to 4.1% (Figure 2). The survey has a very short history, but this is a notably large step down over just one month. We note the level is still too high, and appears to be above BoE forecasts over a comparable period, but there is an increasingly clear downtrend that sends a more convincing message about moderating underlying inflation pressures. GDP growth is running above the BoE<sup>’</sup>s forecasts and this could yet alter the picture, but, for the time being, there remain elements in the data that we believe the BoE could highlight in order to justify an August rate cut.

Business three-year ahead CPI inflation expectations in the DMP remained at 2.6%, comfortably lower than at the start of the year, but yet uncomfortably high given the horizon they are capturing. The CPI data themselves trump all the surveys at present. The biggest question is how isolated the April services surprise was. A repeat of the 1%m/m, sa gain seems highly unlikely in the next release, but a moderation to a 0.3-0.4% pace (our forecast) or lower could still leave the door open for August (assuming other data offer reassurance). A continuation of the 0.4-0.5% pace seen prior to April, however, would present a greater challenge.

The timely RTI payroll based pay data hinted at a national living wage-induced spike in April, as did the pricing component of the PMI. We assume a 0.6%m/m, sa gain in the average weekly earnings figure for April (excluding bonuses, and for the private sector). This would be a stronger gain than the surprise reported in May, and would leave year-ago private sector pay growth at 5.8%. This would leave 2Q pay growth on track to come in line with the BoE<sup>’</sup>s latest forecast. However, we assume a clear moderation after April, and a continuation of the pace seen just before then would surprise the BoE to the upside. We assume a stable unemployment rate and look for signs of a recovery in employment after the weakening in some indicators.

## Emerging Europe

• Poland: NBP keeps rates on hold

• Hungary: Trade surplus hits new highs

• Türkiye: Headline CPI inflation (likely) peaked in May

• Czech Republic: Wages grew faster than expected in 1Q

• Russia: CBR on hold, rhetoric turns more hawkish

## Poland: NBP keeps rates on hold

As widely anticipated, the NBP kept the policy rate unchanged at 5.75% (Figure 1) for the eighth consecutive meeting. The message in the statement is essentially unchanged. Price pressures abated significantly as supply disruptions faded and cost pressures were reduced, growth is improving but still subdued, and this, together with FX strength has brought inflation to levels consistent with the target in 2Q24. However, risks are to the upside because of upcoming increases to regulated energy tariffs as well as fast wage growth, which the statement argues is driven in part by increases in public sector salaries. Based on this the MPC has decided to keep rates unchanged at a level it believes is conducive to bringing inflation to target in the mid-term. We expect two small rates cuts of 25bps each in November and December.

Figure 1: NBP key policy rate
![](images/cbf4452ff9db5cb1f4136986143e5d5f6267dac3ae15df2e8db3d3f625a30884.jpg)

## Hungary: Trade surplus hits new highs

Hungary<sup>’</sup>s trade balance recorded a EUR1.77bn surplus in April (Figure 2), a new record high in the series. There are no obvious seasonal factors in April, so the seasonally-adjusted figure is quite similar. The April surplus, if annualized, is equivalent to over 10% of GDP (Figure 3), matched in history only once (June 2016), and leaving the average surplus of the last three months at 8.4% of GDP. Relative to the previous month, the improvement in the trade balance was due to a 1.7%m/m rise in exports (up EUR0.2 bn to EUR12.5bn) whereas imports were essentially flat at EUR10.7 bn.

The dynamics of the past few months show that exports have been improving somewhat, but imports remain subdued, likely reflecting still weak domestic spending, especially investment, which is the most import-intensive part of domestic demand. We lack for now a breakdown by industry (the final release will be on July 1), but the terms-of-trade boost which drove the big correction in trade is now exhausted, the resilience in the Hungarian surplus, in our view, reflects at this point more the weakness in domestic demand. We expect consumption and in particular investment to recover later this year, and into 2025. Even if we assume Euro Area demand improves somewhat, the trade surplus is likely to shrink towards its 2020-21 levels.

![](images/676671c3d6aee603577fc6227dcf0aca8c759c9ebc9f2dfa305393ba3b486429.jpg)

Figure 3: Hungary trade balance
![](images/ceaf453dd8572268bb64c06be3eb7d316e8efefa8616eb1caa8488cd63f7e05d.jpg)

## Czech Republic: Faster wage growth

Average wage growth stood at 7.1%oya in 1Q24 (Figure 4), clearly stronger than expected (Consensus: 6.0%oya, J.P. Morgan: 6.1%, CNB: 6.4%) and roughly flat from the previous quarter, but only because of a significant upward revision in the 4Q23 figure (to 7%oya, from 6.3% in the original estimate). In fact, the revised series indicates wage growth was lower then previously estimated in 2022 (4.3%oya instead of 5.3%) but higher in 2023 (8%oya, instead of 7.6%). Given the sharp disinflation in consumer prices, the implication is that real wage growth accelerated to 4.8%oya, from -0.5% (Figure 5), after two years of real contraction.

Figure 4: Czech average wages
![](images/57797392279c193741c96e8e58351ab30178c5b8048c9462b41cfd2cdbc8e5ca.jpg)

Figure 5: Czech average wages
![](images/b3243444f8c98fc54a4e6e529f6fadab78cea58a422e06b8c9aad847730ffb1d.jpg)

By sector, the private sector is clearly the driver of wage growth. Private sector average wages were up 8%oya in 1q24, from 7.7% previously, whereas public sector wages slowed to 2%oya, from 3.9% (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Czech average wages - by sector (CNB definition)
![](images/290ac5599e24f10ccd22c1861e656129421c857412fb8944fd0a578245dd14dc.jpg)

Some CNB board members who had once stressed wageprice spiral risks abandoned those concerns over time, or became less vocal about them. But with these data, such considerations are likely to return to the table. We still have an important CPI release (June 11) in the calendar, but absent anything major, we think today<sup>’</sup>s data confirms the next meeting the CNB will reduce the pace of cuts to 25bps, closing the door on a potential 50bp cut. Moreover, if the next two CPI prints continue to show lack of progress in the disinflation process (in line with the past three months, Feb/Mar in particular), we see risks the CNB could deliver a shallower rate cutting cycle than what we have in our baseline (we expect the CNB to proceed in 25bps steps to 4%).

## Türkiye: CPI inflation (likely) peaked in May

Headline CPI inflation came in at 3.4% m/m in May, higher than our expectation and the market consensus of 3.0%. The upside surprise in headline inflation mainly stemmed from services prices. Services prices rose by a strong 4% m/m, while over-year-ago services inflation slowed to 95.9% oya in May, from 97%. Within the services group, restaurant & hotel prices accelerated to 5.5% m/m in May (versus 4.7% in April), while rents rose by a high 5.5% m/m (from 4.6% in April).

Headline CPI inflation increased to 75.4% oya in May, from 69.8% in April, mainly driven by expiry of the natural gas prices subsidy. While energy prices rose by 4.9% m/m, its over-year-ago inflation rate increased from 42.8% in April to 89.7%. We estimate that the mechanical impact of the expiry of natural gas subsidy is 0.7%pt on headline CPI. Core goods prices rose 3.5% m/m, pushing down over-year-ago inflation to 56.5% in May, from 57.1% . Core inflation (C Index; CPI excluding energy, food, beverages, tobacco and gold) rose by a strong 3.8% m/m, leading oya core inflation down to 75.0% in May, 75.8% in April (Figure 7).

Figure 7: Consumer Prices
![](images/146b61ff4777e8f433e4d04785fb44f9b709c194ac77287ea8cdfc9f34423bcc.jpg)

Figure 8: Inflation momentum indicators
![](images/07e9d301e81b0380f0f4b97919c26678e847aa0b2fd598b6e4f7099b7fa4c235.jpg)

Core inflation momentum (3m change, saar, %) remained elevated due to services prices. Seasonally adjusted core infla-

tion (C index) slightly fell to 3.2% m/min May, from 3.3% in April (Figure 8). Services inflation momentum prevented further drop in the inflation momentum. We expect core inflation momentum to improve further through core goods thanks to real lira appreciation in coming months.

Headline inflation likely peaked in May. Turkish lira performed well in May on the back of de-dollarization among Turkish residents and foreign inflows. We continue to see signs of slowdown in economic activity. We expect monthly inflation to come down in summer months thanks to core goods prices driven by real lira appreciation and the slowdown in the economic activity. We do not expect an interim minimum wage hike in July. We believe the worst part of monthly inflation prints is behind us. That said, disinflation is rather slow versus our expectation due to sticky services inflation.

Headline inflation is set to fall sharply towards 50% by August and 43.5% by year-end (Figure 9). We maintain our end-2024 inflation forecast of 43.5% oya and end-2025 inflation forecast of 25.2% oya. We will monitor to what extent real appreciation of lira will offset the effects of automatic interim wage hikes for pensioners and public employees coupled with gradual electricity and natural gas price hikes next month or later this year. Disinflation in services prices has been slower than our expectations in the last three months. We now see upside risks on our inflation forecasts due to sticky services prices. As the CBRT is sensitive to the inflation outlook, we also see upside risks on our year-end policy rate forecast of 45%.

Figure 9: Headline CPI inflation forecasts
![](images/a131b91b46562a9444c719efa01eae5689e8db201856528d9822c8aa677f8e5e.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

## Russia: CBR on hold, for now

The CBR left its policy rate unchanged at last week<sup>’</sup>s meeting, even as the local market was gearing up for a hike. With that said, CBR<sup>’</sup>s rhetoric turned more hawkish, implying a substantial probability of a hike at the next meeting in July. CBR<sup>’</sup>s assessment of inflation risks worsened, and concerns about a lack of slowing in credit activity and the scale of output gap increased. More constructively, assessment of fiscal risks remained unchanged — consolidation is expected to continue into 2025 — while the jump in wage growth in 1Q was linked to one-off bonus payments. The CBR now believes that monetary policy will have to stay restrictive for a more extended period.

Our interpretation is that the CBR took more time to assess the economic/inflation backdrop following the substantial tightening of monetary policy of 2H23. Although the board<sup>’</sup>s growing dissatisfaction about lack of progress on disinflation front is palpable, the recent data flow looks more mixed, suggesting that tighter policy may finally be filtering into the system. April growth data was soft, while the composite PMI signaled further downshift in growth in 2Q, even as labor market and inflation still looked problematic.

By the July meeting, we<sup>’</sup>ll get another batch of growth data and two CPI prints, which may sway the decision either way. Our intuition is that by end-July, interpretations of growth picture will be less rosy — although the labor market will still be seen as very tight — while measures of underlying inflation will moderate a tad. This should bring back the sense that things are at least moving in the right direction, even if not as fast as previously planned. Our base case is therefore that further hikes will be avoided, albeit the key rate will have to remain at current level for a longer period than previously thought. We now see the first cut only late in 2024, with rate ending the year at 15% vs. 13.5% previously.

## Czech Republic:

<table><tr><td>Tue</td><td colspan="5">Consumer prices</td></tr><tr><td>Jun 11</td><td>%oya</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="5">9:00am</td><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>2.0 0.3</td><td>2.0 0.1</td><td>2.9 0.7</td><td>2.7 0.1</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m nsa Food</td><td>-4.8</td><td>-5.9</td><td>-2.7</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Housing</td><td>3.4</td><td>3.5</td><td>3.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>1.1</td><td>3.5</td><td>4.7</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Transport</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr></table>

We estimate that headline CPI slowed to 2.7%oya in May (from 2.9%), driven mainly by lower food inflation. Core inflation however is likely to have stayed flat at 2.6%oya which, if confirmed, would mark the first month of no declines after 18 consecutive months in which core prints came successively lower

Source: NBH , Local sources, J.P. Morgan forecasts

Hungarian CPI likely accelerated to 4.1%oya in May (from 3.7% in April)m which if confirmed would break a streak of four months inside the NBH<sup>’</sup>s target tolerance band (3%oya +/-1%-pt). The increase comes as base effects in food, fuel and regulated items are no longer favourable. Core inflation is likely to fall further but staying at elevated levels (core CPI at 3.9%oya, core excluding all food at 5.4%).

We expect Israel<sup>’</sup>s inflation momentum to moderate in May after a few one-offs pulled it higher in March-April, but risks to our forecast are skewed to the upside.

We expect the final release to confirm May inflation at 2.5%oya (up from 2.4% in April), as in the preliminary release. The details are likely to show continued resilience in services prices accompanied by subdued prices action in core goods, supported by a strong zloty.

Most hard data on Russian growth in April came in on the soft side of expectations. In fact, most indicators except for construction displayed negative m/m, sa, dynamics, with the index of basic sectors, which covers more than half of the economy, contracting 0.7% on the month, we estimate. This implies slight downside risk to our 1.25%q/q, saar, 2Q GDP forecast and we<sup>’</sup>ll need some bounce in activity in May-June for our forecast to realize. In the meantime, business surveys point to healthy dynamics in manufacturing, but a visible slowdown in services in May. Business and consumer confidence remain at high levels. Labor market tightened further in April, with unemployment reaching all-time low of 2.6%, sa, and nominal wage growth accelerating above 20%oya in March.

## South Africa

• GDP contracted by 0.1%q/q in 1Q amid weak domestic consumption

GDP should rebound in 2Q led by IP; we look for 0.9%y/y full year growth

• Attempt at unity government hit a snag

We revise CAD forecast to 2% of GDP (from 2.2%) this year

GDP contracted 0.1%q/q (-0.3% saar) in 1Q24, landing below our and consensus forecasts of a 0.1% gain as domestic demand weakened. There was also an upward revision to 4Q (up a tenth to +0.3%q/q) though not sufficient to make up for the downside surprise. Instead, agriculture came in a bit softer, while expenditure details disappointed. Nonetheless, the GDP report affirms our view that inflation (particularly core) should remain contained in the near term. In this sense, we keep seeing monetary policy easing by 3Q this year (baring adverse global developments) as we also moderate our GDP forecast for this year by a tenth to 0.9%y/y due to the downside surprise. We continue to see 2Q GDP rising 0.6%q/q (2.5%q/q saar) as IP should show some payback underpinned by stable power supply.

Figure 1: HCE and wage income growth %oya, 2QMA
![](images/7c57cbd125e12fbfe189d89aac57c1eec482e7b6634eb3785628c3d74cd929a5.jpg)
Source: StatsSA, J.P.Morgan calculations

## Elections: Attempt at unity government hit a snag

The ANC<sup>’</sup>s top decision making body this week affirmed its commitment to seek a government of national unity (GNU), after recent elections saw it lose its majority. While a GNU avoids the criticism of favoring one opposition party and ideology over another, we had deemed such a formation as not necessarily workable. Indeed, in the aftermath of the ANC<sup>’</sup>s announcements, the self-described radical EFF party ruled out participation in the GNU, objecting to the potential participation of the DA party. While its stance may well soften, indications are that a minority government with a supply and confidence agreement with the DA may well be a more likely outcome. Negotiations over the next week will be at a crucial stage and most options are still on the table. Yet, there is a very tight timeline as the first sitting of Parliament needs to occur by 18 June.

## Revising ‘24 CAD up to 2.0% of GDP

The 1Q24 current account deficit (CAD) narrowed to 1.2% of GDP, below the consensus of 1.6% of GDP and a touch higher than our call of -0.9% of GDP. The compression from 2.3% of GDP in 4Q was chiefly driven by a wider trade surplus of 2.6% of GDP (from 1.5%) supported by better terms of trade (Figure 1). In addition, import volumes contracted 5.1%q/q, while that of exports dipped only 2.3%. Yet, the deficit on the invisible balances (income, transfers and services) widened to 3.7%, entirely driving the miss relative to our projections. In turn, the primary income deficit expanded further to 2.2% of GDP (from 1.8%) probably due to dividend outflows.

Figure 2: South Africa - Trade balance and terms of trade
![](images/91191dbe7ec85e16e50a271582fcf0a1f03d64a9961df87402d062304f8533aa.jpg)

We revise up our CAD forecast from 2.2% to 2.0%. Our new baseline envisage a sustained trade surplus in 2Q (smaller at 1.5% of GDP, but higher than our previous forecast of 0.6%) as commodity prices remain supportive. In addition, mining activity probably is solid this quarter buoyed by stable power supply. Yet, our forecast balances against a likely uptick in imports as vehicles shipment (that largely underpinned the imports contraction in 1Q) began recovering in April (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Monthly capital goods imports
![](images/f1964d2788edf1336cd9b7a42ac41356a2c6775000b218e6f9a8c878a76e67e5.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, J.P. Morgan
