## SSA Focus: Zambia’s BoP looks manageable

The recent grants funding pledges, elevated copper prices, IMF programme augmentation, and the progress on debt negations augur well for dollar liquidity in Zambia. We now see a US\$\$0.2bn funding gap this year (down from US\$0.6bn previously) even as the ongoing drought weighs on external balances. Indeed, our baseline forecast points to a 1.8% of GDP current account deficit (CAD) this year (from -0.3% in 2023) as the trade surplus is likely smaller. Lower copper output and higher imports should narrow the trade surplus to 2.6% of GDP (from 4.7%), notwithstanding strong sensitivity of exports to copper prices. In addition, the invisibles gap (income and services) probably is wider as Zambia resumes servicing its external debt.

Figure 1: Zambia copper prices and production
![](images/7820c5d41ac155ca5bb32b18e0a4a22c0ee8bdaa17dabbe9bf98630df96c5460.jpg)

Recent newsflow should temper concerns around Zambia<sup>’</sup>s large funding needs this year. The protracted debt negotiations following a default in 2021 had constricted external funding options to plug the estimated US\$0.9bn budget gap due to the drought. Yet, reports suggest bondholders voted in favour of a restructuring deal. This follows multi-lateral and bilateral partners pledging US\$0.5bn in grants, with the IMF looking to augment the ECF/EFF programme by an additional 30% of quota (amounting to c.US\$0.4bn).

However, external imbalances remain wide. The trade surplus likely is halved to 2.6% of GDP as the drought constrains mining activity and raises imports. Zambia relies on hydroelectric power, accounting for 85% of total power generation. Falling water levels resulted in miners being asked to ration power by up to 25%. This is similar to the 2015/16 drought episode when copper production fell 6%y/y. Our baseline assumes a 3% drop in copper output this time as, in contrast to 2015/16, higher copper prices allow miners to absorb the costs of alternative energy sources. Yet, higher imports of energy and food, with net exports of agricultural products sliding on draught, will pressure the trade balance.

Our trade balance forecast assumes a 12%y/y average rise in copper prices to US\$9.5k/t this year, as per the JPM houseview. Shocking our baseline for a further 10% price increase improves the trade balance by 1.7%pt to 4.3% of GDP, even with flat output. The impact on CAD should be smaller in that scenario, hardly challenging our expectation for a widening current account deficit (CAD) this year – the country<sup>’</sup>s invisibles deficit (income and services) would increase on dividends payments as mines are largely foreign owned.

While higher input costs should lower dividend payments this year, the invisibles gap should nevertheless increase to around 4.4% of GDP as Zambia resumes servicing its external debt. We look for a1.8% of GDP C/A deficit this year, from -0.3% in 2023 (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Zambia current account dynamics
![](images/80f277716e615644eb64d06e58f57aa0556b3742e9395ba000f3fbbe3161382d.jpg)
Source: BOZ, J.P. Morgan

When we project external financing requirements from the C/ A and debt amortisations against likely financing sources, we are left with a BoP gap of US\$0.2bn this year. FDI inflows along the mining supply chain will probably improve thanks to higher copper prices), even as higher energy costs will remain a constraint. With portfolio flows also unlikely to pick up, and grants probably insufficient, added funding support from other multi-lateral and bilateral partners remain crucial to plug the shortfall (Table 1).

Table 1: Zambia external financing needs and sources

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australian real GDP increased 0.1%q/q in 1Q24, a bit less than expected

But household consumption, which the RBA is placing weight on, was revised up in prior quarters

• Australia<sup>’</sup>s minimum wage will increase 3.75%y/y in FY25, below the average of the past two years

We expect Australia’s jobless rate will fall 0.1%-pt to 4% in May

Australian 1Q GDP increased 0.1%q/q, below both J.P. Morgan (0.3%q/q) and consensus (0.2%q/q) forecasts. The composition of growth was consistent with the partial data released beforehand (Figure 1). Weak net trade (-0.9%-pts) was largely offset by strong inventory accumulation (+0.7%-pts), government and private consumption both contributed 0.2%-pts to aggregate GDP, while business capex imparted a drag of 0.2%-pts.

The ABS has reintroduced the National Visitor Survey and updated data reveal the number of Australians traveling abroad in the past 18 months has been materially greater than first thought. As a result, the net trade contribution since 2022 is now much smaller than previously reported. However, in line with the global convention, expenditures by Australian residents travelling abroad are added to household final consumption, delivering a non-trivial upshift in private spending.

Figure 1: Domestic demand and real imports
![](images/0112dca75ce26a792f5d9d2543d554b41528f9fca32fba98aa043efdec4dc08b.jpg)

From an aggregate perspective, these dynamics are offsetting and don<sup>’</sup>t have a huge impact on GDP (consumption stronger, net trade weaker, Figure 1); however, this mix does boost 2023 domestic demand growth by 0.5%-pts and suggests a more resilient local economy. Resilience is perhaps most evident in the household consumption numbers where last year<sup>’</sup>s growth rate was revised materially higher from 1.1%y/y to 2.1%y/y. The sequentials are still weak/unimpressive, but these changes are meaningful and indicate the household sector is stronger than previously thought.

This is important for the RBA given the most recent minutes highlighted that the weakness in consumption was an important consideration in the decision to remain on hold, rather than hike. We don<sup>’</sup>t think these revisions are enough to see the RBA lift rates again, but the shift in composition skews hawkish when viewed through the lens of a stronger consumer and still elevated inflation. We retain the view that the RBA will stay on hold this year, with cuts most likely from early 2025.

## Minimum wage increase normalizing

Australia<sup>’</sup>s Fair Work Commission has announced the award and minimum wage will increase 3.75%y/y in FY2025. This is below the average of the past two years (5.5%y/y) and the broader wage price index (4.1%oya), but still higher than the immediate pre-COVID average (3.3%y/y). Award/minimum wage agreements are reset annually so legislative outcomes are routinely captured in the wage price index seasonal adjustment process. Therefore, what matters most for estimating the impulse to aggregate wage growth is strength/weakness versus the longer-run average (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Change in award/min. wage relative to 5yr average
![](images/6acdd0d5d63492796ab10419effe877eb69be7879d11f4cce676a11fc3d19f95.jpg)

Importantly, the large legislated wage increases in the past few years are now inflating the rolling five-year average, which in our view will water down the impulse from the FY25 increase to the seasonally adjusted WPI print. Indeed, applying the appropriate sector weights we estimate that today<sup>’</sup>s outcome will be neutral for aggregate wage growth, with a contribution very close to zero. For context, last year<sup>’</sup>s decision added \~0.5%-pts over and above the normal award/ minimum wage impulse.

While the RBA doesn<sup>’</sup>t provide explicit award/minimum wage estimates, today<sup>’</sup>s outcome appears broadly aligned with the central bank<sup>’</sup>s WPI forecast. Accordingly, we don<sup>’</sup>t think this outcome will move the needle on the Bank<sup>’</sup>s inflation/wage forecast or change its thinking on current monetary policy settings. We retain the view for cash rate stability this year, with rate cuts likely from early 2025.

## Unemployment rate retracing

We expect Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate fell a tenth to 4% in May, alongside a steady participation rate and a 20K increase in employment. The 0.2%-pt jump in the unemployment rate in April appeared to reflect changing seasonality to some extent, with the ABS noting that part of the rise in unemployed reflected, “more people than usual indicating that they had a job they were waiting to start in.<sup>”</sup> A similar dynamic was evident in the rise in the jobless rate in January, and the increase was subsequently unwound in February. We also anticipate some retracement in the unemployment rate in the May numbers, although the ABS notes that the increase in marginally attached workers only accounts for some of the rise in the unemployment rate in April, so we don<sup>’</sup>t expect a complete reversal.

Figure 3: Australia average hours per worker and unemployment
![](images/46eb573b00d70910123391e214a32b8b072583430d3922e58a5bd0c87df372b5.jpg)

Our broad sense is that an unemployment rate in the high 3s-4% is about right, although seasonality issues have made it hard to glean information about the direction of travel from month to month changes. As we noted last week, the recent recovery in the broader labor market indicators (e.g. hours worked, Figure 3) imply the labor market stabilized in 1Q24, following the increase in slack in late 2023. Upgrades to domestic demand in this week<sup>’</sup>s GDP revisions also help to explain some of the resilience in the labor data in the past year, although the jobless rate is still tracking too low relative to domestic demand on an historical basis.

## Greater China

• China: Caixin PMI moved up to a two-year high

• Trade report in line with expectations

• Taiwan: May PMI beat expectations

Headline CPI came in higher-than-expected on food and energy, while core CPI stayed benign

• Exports below expectations, led by tech sector easing

• Next week: China inflation and credit, Taiwan CBC

China<sup>’</sup>s May Caixin manufacturing PMI moved up 0.3-pt to 51.7, slightly beating market expectations. This is the fourth consecutive month of improvement in the Caixin manufacturing PMI, hitting the highest in the past two years. The improvement stands in contrast to the decline in NBS manufacturing PMI (which fell 0.9-pt to 49.5). This is not unusual. In fact, NBS and Caixin manufacturing PMI readings have moved in opposite directions in nine out the past 12 months (Figure 1). This can be attributable to the differences in sampling methods (Caixin PMI is biased towards export-oriented smaller enterprises) and statistical approaches (different seasonal adjustment approaches), but may also point to uncertainties in the economic outlook at enterprise levels.

Figure 1: Monthly change in China manufacturing PMIs
![](images/b24a962571ce28660da33c800cd470009217e96b828cba18de4814e3d56638bc.jpg)
Source: NBS, Caixin, J.P. Morgan

Comparing the May manufacturing PMI readings from Caixin and NBS, the common messages are: (1) the output component remained at above 50; (2) new order and export order components declined; (3) the employment component improved; (4) input and output price components picked up.

The near-term softness is mainly related to the ongoing weakness in the housing market (which is a drag on real estate investment and housing-related consumption), moderation in consumption activity and exports after a strong start in 1Q. The policy adjustment since late April will be useful to manage the downside risk to economic activity, but may not change the trend of a near-term slowdown. We maintain our 2Q GDP growth forecast of 3.5%q/q saar (vs. 7.4% in 1Q).

## Trade report in line with expectations

China<sup>’</sup>s exports remained steady in May following the strong growth in Jan-Apr, growing 7.6%oya with a modest 0.9%m/ m sa fall (Figure 2). By product breakdown, export performance is not much different across products, with a 0.3%m/m sa marginal gain in low-end consumer goods, and a 0.5% and 0.7% fall in mechanical & electrical products and high-tech products. Auto exports fell 4.9%. By destination, May exports to EM countries (Russia: +7.8%m/m sa, LatAm: +8.8%) significantly outperformed exports to the US (-3.5%) and EU (-1.8%).

Figure 2: China merchandise exports and imports US\$ billion,sa, 3mma
![](images/cf023f4dbb79de0d025ad094d01aa05b2a4de21b7c8f69099eb0763c2f7fcd12.jpg)

Nominal imports came in roughly in line with expectations, rising 1.8%oya or 0.2%m/m sa in May, led by EM Asia (3.3%m/m sa) but dragged by imports from the US (-8.2%), EU (-4.5%) and Brazil (-5.3%). Imports of commodities turned out to be mixed, with rising imports in crude oil (6.9%m/m sa), coal (5.0%) and copper (18.4%) but declining imports in steel products (-6.5%) and iron ore (-0.5%), mostly a reversal of April movements.

Exports have been a stable growth driver this year. The trade surplus registered US\$338.3bn in Jan-May, the highest on record for the same period. The improvement in global final demand, along with the strength of Chinese exports (the scale of production and lower export prices), jointly lifted China<sup>’</sup>s export volume by 12.2%oya (average in Jan-Apr).

Chinese exporters<sup>’</sup> diversification efforts across products and destinations may continue to support export performance in the near term. Nonetheless, risk may intensify on exports going forward. The rise of protectionism, the wider use of tariffs, 301 investigations, export controls, investment restrictions and sanctions, may lead to further distortion or disruption to the international trade system and add pressure for the Chinese economy. In particular, Trump<sup>’</sup>s threat to hike tariffs on Chinese goods to 60% (and to 10% globally) poses a major risk into 2025 and beyond. Under that scenario, it may trigger significant depreciation of CNY against the USD, acceleration of global supply chain relocation, and China<sup>’</sup>s

retaliatory measures (e.g. retaliatory tariffs and export controls on strategically important minerals). Still, it is not easy to decouple from China, hence the process of global supply chain relocation may be lengthy.

## FX reserves rose US\$31.2bn

China<sup>’</sup>s May FX reserves beat market expectations, rising US\$31.2bn to \$3,232.0bn (Figure 3). This partially offsets the sharp \$44.8bn fall in April. Gold reserves stayed unchanged, after solid increases in the prior 18 consecutive months. With an elevated trade surplus, we expect the current account surplus for goods and services moved up to \$24.6bn (vs. \$15.9bn in April). Meanwhile, the US dollar index eased from 106.2 to 104.7 in May, leading to an estimated currency valuation gain of US\$19.6bn. Taken together, we tentatively estimate a US\$13.0bn implied capital outflow in May (vs. US\$-38.1bn in April).

Figure 3: Monthly change in China's FX reserves
![](images/442e38aa722cd80cab6380bd8883dd8acdacf240625eed5bb9d328d5ac8c1359.jpg)

Last week, the SAFE disclosed that they have lifted the QDII quota to 167.8bn by end of May, up by US\$2.3bn compared to one year ago. Demand for QDII products has been strong. Most products had a very high subscription rate, and thereby a purchase limit for investors. That is partly due to more attractive investment returns. Our strategist estimates QDII funds have returned on average (weighted by AUM) around 6.9% to investors year-to-date, compared to an uninspiring 1% from non-QDII mutual funds onshore. The incremental addition in QDII quota marks the PBOC<sup>’</sup>s efforts to open more room for official channels to facilitate overseas investments, though the capacity to digest the accumulated capital outflow pressure seems limited.

We think tariffs are becoming more of a concern for CNY. Near-term focus is on the EU tariff on China EV exports, which likely will be announced on June 10. Our auto analysts estimate that China NEV exports to Belgium account for 11.4% of China<sup>’</sup>s total NEV exports, 8.0% for England, 5.6% for Spain, 2-3% for Netherland, Germany and France. Although the EU<sup>’</sup>s tariff increase on EV will likely be much smaller than the 75%pts hike from the US, the impact could

be larger.

For the rest of the year and beyond, the upcoming US election and potential 60% tariff on Chinese goods if Trump is reelected or a potential trade war 2.0 will be essential for CNY movements. The 2017-2018 trade war suggests around 1% CNY depreciation against USD in response to a 2% tariff increase. Extrapolating the relationship to a 60% tariff scenario suggests around 15% depreciation for CNY. However, the relationship could go non-linear. On the one hand, the CNY may depreciate less because: (1) China<sup>’</sup>s share in US imports is ⅓ less and US share in China imports in nearly ¼ less; (2) actual average tariff may be lower than 60%; and (3) the PBOC likely will let the CNY depreciate, but USD/CNY may still face strong defense at important levels (e.g. 7.50, 7.80 or 8.0). On the other hand, if the tariff war 2.0 involves spillover of tariff increase by other trading partners or US tariff increase on goods produced in a third country by Chinese companies, the impact could be more disruptive, and the implications on CNY will be more complicated.

## Taiwan: May PMI beat expectations

After tracking a significant downward trend throughout 2022, Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing PMI have been volatile with moderate upward trend (from subdued level) since early 2023. The momentum to trend up seems to have accelerated this year. In May, headline manufacturing PMI rose another 0.7-pt to 50.9 (Figure 4), adding to the 0.9-pt gain in April, the highest reading since May 2022.

Figure 4: Taiwan manufacturing PMI output and IP
![](images/e3049e9fec74e280cc48be03772e94cf511b93b0d1a55b14cfb0ce531fa76e30.jpg)

The latest signals from Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing PMI have continued to be encouraging. The solid gain in the output component (up 1.2-pt to 52.6 in May) hints at solid near-term momentum in industrial production activity. Regarding external demand conditions, signals from the manufacturing PMI export orders component have been rather benign with a tick lower by 0.1-pt to 49.2 in May. It remains the highest reading since March 2023, hinting at a constructive near-term outlook on external demand. Meanwhile, regarding the latest trend in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s inventory cycle, the manufacturing PMI finished

2011 2012 2013 2014 2015 2016 2017 2018 2019 2020 2021 2022 2023 2024 Source: DGBAS, J.P. Morgan

goods inventory indicator fell 1.2-pt to 48.9 in May, partly offsetting the 2.2-pt gain in April. As such, the manufacturing PMI order to inventory ratio came in at 1.05 in May (compared to Jan-Apr average at 1.01).

## CPI rose 2.2%oya on food and energy

After delivering downside surprise for two consecutive months in March and April, Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s CPI inflation beat expectations in May. Headline CPI rose 2.2%oya or 0.2%m/ m sa (Figure 5). The monthly gain was most evident in energy, up 0.9%m/m sa. It reflects the continuing impact of the recent electricity price hike. Meanwhile, food prices rose 3.4%oya or 0.2%m/m sa, mainly driven by vegetable and fruit price increases due to the late April rainstorm and supply disruptions. Core CPI remained benign, rising 1.8%oya or 0.1%m/m sa. Excluding vegetable and fruit, May CPI rose 1.9%oya.

Figure 5: Taiwan headline and core CPI %oya
![](images/ab75ae655414af0c0bf0a3e35692ea97780d4265474bc11bbc8967a3eaed4590.jpg)

The downside surprises in April CPI, the benign May CPI (if short-term food price seasonality is excluded), and the general easing in underlying inflation trends are encouraging. Looking ahead, assuming some moderate indirect effect of the electricity price hike in the coming months, we expect Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s headline CPI inflation to stay around 2% in the middle part of the year, before easing to around 1.5% into the year-end (especially considering the higher base effect in 2H23). Overall, we expect headline CPI inflation to average 1.9% in 2024, and core CPI inflation to average at 1.8%.

The CBC<sup>’</sup>s March monetary policy meeting minutes also raised concerns about stickiness in prices for food away from home and rental prices. In this regard, price increases for food away from home have eased lately, while rental CPI ticked up to 2.6%oya in May. Taken together, we maintain our baseline call, looking for the Taiwan central bank to keep policy rates on hold in the upcoming June CBC monetary policy meeting next week.

## Exports below expectations on tech easing

Following solid sequential growth trend in 4Q23 and 1Q24, Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s trade reports for April and May showed some moderation in export activity. Merchandise exports came in below expectations again in May, rising 3.5%oya. Seasonally adjusted, May exports fell 2.5% m/m sa, adding to the decline of 7.6% in April, with the sequential trend growing moderately at 5.9% 3m/3m saar. Adjusted for export prices, real exports fell 2.1% m/m sa.

Tech exports fell 4.2% m/m sa in May, adding to decline of 8.3% m/ sa in April, with the sequential trend still showing moderate growth at 6.5% 3m/3m saar (Figure 6). Further breakdown shows some easing in exports of information and communication products, suggesting the significant rally in AI-related exports in recent quarters may be moderating somewhat in the near term. Breakdown by export destinations suggests near-term easing in exports to the US following significant out-performance in recent quarters. Exports to other major DM markets (Europe and Japan), as well as exports to China/ HKSAR, continue to show softer underlying growth trends.

Figure 6: Taiwan export breakdown by products US\$ bn, sa, 3mma
![](images/c45508fa342581c23d605d0fa53b16e8282d10927d81dbb8eb8bfd66f6408c75.jpg)

Going forward, we look for steady upward trend in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s tech exports in the coming quarters, including structural expansion in exports of AI-related products and HPC-related demand growth, though the recent easing in tech export momentum is worth monitoring. The recent sequential upturn in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s capital goods imports is encouraging, suggesting tentative recovery in the domestic capex trend, likely reflecting Taiwan manufacturers<sup>’</sup> improving sentiment on the export sector outlook.

## Korea

• Customs exports fell in May, yet three-month trend growth held up

Manufacturing PMI rebounded strongly to a two-year high level

• May CPI inflation was softer than expected

The most recent manufacturing sector data were encouraging. April IP turned up as expected and the manufacturing PMI rebounded strongly. While May customs exports were a bit of disappointment, falling after April<sup>’</sup>s gain, trend growth held up to suggest positive growth in 2Q as a whole. The sector details were mixed across the production and exports data, yet we still expect the tech sector will take a lead in manufacturing sector growth throughout 2024 on upbeat demand growth for AI-related high value-added memory chips. Meanwhile, May CPI data was softer than expected, which should provide reassurance for the Bank of Korea on its inflation forecast path through 2024. We believe that the CPI data is consistent with our view of policy rate cuts to start in 4Q24.

## Customs exports fell in May on non-tech

Nominal customs exports fell 1.1%m/m, sa, in May after a 4.3% gain in April, a disappointment compared to the flash 20-day series<sup>’</sup> gain by 1.9%. The three-month trend growth marked 2.7%3m/3m, saar, to modestly recover after an 0.8% contraction in March, and the 2Q exports growth is expected to turn up modestly after 1Q<sup>’</sup>s contraction.

Figure 1: Korea customs exports - tech vs. nontech
![](images/aa3b0e1a79b759ca9fbb8cbf334f8174877b034195524de4cf5d53b4e07d333b.jpg)

In the details, non-tech exports fell to offset the gain in tech exports. Non-tech exports fell 3.5%m/m, sa, in May after a 4.7% gain in April, and the non-tech exports<sup>’</sup> three-month trend nominal growth remained sluggish at -3.9%3m/3m, saar. Meanwhile, tech exports rose 4.7%m/m, sa, in May on top of the 3.5% gain in April, as semiconductor (2.5%) and mobile device (14.8%) exports rose. The three-month trend growth of tech exports remained strong at 20.4%3m/3m, saar (Figure 1). We have noted from April IP reports that the tech (notably memory chip) sector should lead the overall manufacturing sector growth in 2024, and May customs trade data is in line with our long-standing view.

## Manufacturing PMI rebounded strongly

Manufacturing PMI rose strongly by 2.2pts to 51.6 in May, fully reversing a cumulative 1.8pt drop in the prior three months, and marked the highest rise since May 2022. The key sub-indices of output and new orders also rose to 34- and 27- month highs, respectively. The headline PMI<sup>’</sup>s strong rebound with the resilient key details conforms to the actual outturns: IP sequentially turned to positive growth in April, after a temporary contraction in February and March.

Figure 2: Korea manufacturing output - PMI vs. IP
![](images/bddfd84f5a74b26404fb8a9a8bd12d625b6c5380cb328a84d4a90974960c73da.jpg)

In May, the output PMI jumped up 3.2pts to 53.3, marking the largest monthly gain since September 2020. Its current level implies IP growth of modestly above 10%ar, more upbeat than actual IP which just recovered to +2.0%3m/3m, saar, in April from -1.1% in March. We expect 2Q24<sup>’</sup>s IP growth to be around 5%q/q, saar, a bit lower than the PMIimplied levels (but still resilient) (Figure 2). Meanwhile, the new orders index also rose strongly by 2.6pts to 52.9. The export orders index rose by 1.2pts to 51.9, the second-highest level in the past two years,continuing to suggest solid growth in exports.

## CPI inflation was softer than expected

CPI inflation stepped down further to 2.7%oya in May to modestly undershoot expectations (JPMe 2.9%, consensus 2.8%), from 2.9% in April and 3.1% in March. If seasonally adjusted, the headline CPI rose 0.1%m/m, sa and 1.9% 3m/3m, saar to suggest an ongoing stable trend around the target growth. Excluding food and energy products, core CPI rose 2.2%oya, and seasonally adjusted core CPI rose 0.1%m/ m, sa and 2.3%3m/3m, saar.

In the details, oil product prices rose 1.0%m/m, sa in May after falling for two months to reflect the temporary rise in global crude oil prices in April. But we think they are set to resume a monthly fall in June given the global oil prices

downward trend since April. Fresh farm product prices were weaker than our expectation of a fall of 1.0%m/m, sa for the second monthly drop to correct after strong rises in 2023- 1Q24. Core goods/service prices continued the stable trend growth. Core goods (industrial products excluding oil products) prices rose 0.2%m/m, sa and 1.5%3m/3m, saar, and private service prices rose 0.3%m/m, sa and 2.4%3m/3m, saar.

Week of June 10 - 14
Figure 3: Inflation forecast
![](images/cc1307666254816c985110a1a326a9ab8307dfd37ee8c683fea1c18cc265bdcf.jpg)
Source: NSO and J.P. Morgan

Looking forward, we expect the headline %oya inflation to remain at the upper-2% pace (around 2.7%oya level) for two more months, then to fall towards the 2% target from August. Oil and utility prices are likely to remain as wild cards, yet we see a higher possibility of approaching the 2% target by end-2024 given the stable trend in core prices (Figure 3).

## ASEAN

• Thailand<sup>’</sup>s growth outlook has improved on manufacturing and fiscal spending recovery

Inflation set to return to target range, which should keep the BoT on hold in 2H24

We revise 2024 CA surplus forecast lower again on higher oil/shipping costs and weak travel spending

Thailand<sup>’</sup>s growth outlook has improved as manufacturing activity picks up and fiscal spending rises. Inflation is also set to return to target starting May, with higher energy and food price pressures on the rise. With stronger growth-inflation dynamics, we expect the Bank of Thailand (BoT) to stay on hold, although political interference remains a background risk. We revise our 2024 CA surplus forecast lower again, given potentially higher oil/shipping prices through the summer and still weak travel spending.

## Sunnier disposition

After a series of disappointing prints, 1Q24 GDP (1.5%oya) posted a significant positive surprise (JPM: 0.4%oya, consensus: 0.8%oya), with an above-trend sequential expansion of 4.6%q/q, saar. The primary reason was the stronger-than-expected rebound in private consumption and services, even as fiscal spending, fixed asset investment, and exports remained tepid, due to the delayed FY24 budget process and the continued drag from manufacturing de-stocking.

Going forward, we think that the economy has bottomed out and a cyclical lift is in the cards. The key growth catalyst is the manufacturing sector. After over a year of de-stocking, excess capacity is much less of an issue now (Figure 1). This should lead to a re-coupling of manufacturing activity and external demand. The auto sector is also poised to benefit from Japan<sup>’</sup>s recovery from the temporary plant shutdowns in 1Q24, which should help to normalize the supply of intermediate goods and capital goods. The latest PMI readings have affirmed this positive outlook so far, with the new order-to-inventory ratio rising sharply (Figure 2).

Figure 1: Thailand manufacturing sector
![](images/692c3a9b5be9c916e2b318f8ab455e0ea1abfd4e9cefa4f217ccdf1543f98ef0.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

Figure 2: Thailand manufacturing PMI
![](images/440270fd3f56b9267063240cf2412a71b97169b0bf9ecee1c239973933e0e627.jpg)

Fiscal disbursements are also set to accelerate in the second half of FY24 (ending September), following the belated parliamentary budget approval in end-March. The catch-up in spending will be more pronounced in new public investment projects (Figure 3) with higher multipliers and fiscal impulse.

Figure 3: Public spending - contribution to headline GDP Pt. contribution to %oya Consumption
![](images/59b9efb0c316b2a40191399662702c66c6bc5b3f2355cc32887011ad3cf75ffe.jpg)

The private consumption picture is more nuanced (Figure 4). While stable labor market conditions and the ongoing tourism recovery will help sustain consumer spending in basic staples and services, discretionary expenditure (e.g., autos, housing) will likely continue to bear the brunt of tighter bank lending standards and an elevated household debt servicing burden.

Figure 4: Thailand private consumption indicator (PCI)
![](images/d2758d7cf77f49f70f65e1ef9e226bb8ce552d3e28b315c51987f5613d2b75c4.jpg)
Considering these catalysts, we recently revised up our 2024 GDP estimate from 2.3% to 2.7%, with another strong quarter (4.8%q/q, saar) in store for 2Q24 before growth momentum moderates to trend levels (around 3%) in 2H24.

## Inflation back to target; BoT to stay on hold

Following a spate of negative prints since 4Q23, the inflation outlook is also on the cusp of a sharp turnaround. On the energy front, authorities are ramping up the speed of diesel price hikes via the expiry of fuel excise tax cuts and rationalization of Oil Fund subsidies (Figure 5), while prospects of a crude oil rally in the summer could also lead to higher unsubsidized pump prices. The recent heat wave has also affected agricultural output and pushed up wholesale prices (Figure 6), although the pass-through to retail prices depends on micro factors such as pricing power. We expect headline CPI to return to the 1%-3% target range in May and rise to a peak of 2.0%-2.5% through end-4Q24 and early-1Q25.

Figure 5: Thailand high-speed diesel retail price structure
![](images/caa869c47455a7e3de077489f8f87c37c4fd925d24d648a3c302313771246132.jpg)
Source: EPPO, J.P. Morgan

Figure 6: Thailand agri production vs. agri PPI vs. raw food CPI Jan 2010 = 100
![](images/35ad1ae5227fed0257c8493363522bdf451459802a55686aa05f334723506351.jpg)

With stronger growth prospects and the return of inflation to target, the macro case for policy easing in 2H24 has diminished. In a policy review exercise, the Bank of Thailand (BoT) has also decided to maintain the 1%-3% inflation target, while recent official comments continue to suggest that financial stability goals (i.e., household debt deleveraging) take precedence in the reaction function, absent a negative growth or inflation shock. We continue to expect the central bank to be on hold through 2H24.

Nonetheless, political intervention in monetary policy remains a background risk. There are reports (see link) that the government will push for the appointment of a governor that is more sympathetic with its views. However, the current governor<sup>’</sup>s term only ends in September 2025, so the risk of political interference in the near term is likely contained.

## CA still under pressure

The April current account (CA) fell to a minor deficit of US\$0.04 billion (March: US\$1.1 billion), on the back of weaker goods and services balances. Although we continue to expect a backloaded CA recovery, there are still downside risks due to cyclical and structural reasons. If materialized, another oil price rally (US\$10pbl higher by September), as forecasted by our commodities strategists, will place renewed pressure on the energy balance. Port congestion in China is also leading to a spike in global shipping costs and depending on the duration of the supply-side disruption, is also CA-negative for a major freight service importer such as Thailand.

The bright spot is the steady improvement in tourism, driven almost exclusively by China (Figure 7). Year-to-date arrivals have reached 12.1mn (2023: 8.7mn) and are on track to hitting 35mn for the whole year (88% of 2019 level). However, as highlighted before, the quality of spending remains weak vis-à-vis pre-pandemic levels (Figure 8) and will continue to be a drag on the CA recovery going into 2025.

Considering these risks, we revise lower our 2024 CA surplus forecast again from 1.3% of GDP to 0.6% of GDP (Table 1), with the bulk of the recovery backloaded to 4Q24.

Figure 7: Thailand foreign arrivals
![](images/a575d0c8f88690a03068d55b738e4c470fe19529a55babc29cb64b1978897fd2.jpg)

Figure 8: Thailand gross travel receipt US\$ billion
![](images/462ef88ce59c2bd1130fe15258bb6f537c07235fc9c75b0ae3106a796a2550d8.jpg)

## India

• BJP to form coalition government

• RBI on hold, as expected

• Global economy, fiscal and monsoon outcomes to shape the outlook

India<sup>’</sup>s Monetary Policy Committee expectedly stayed on hold and retained its “withdrawal of accommodation<sup>”</sup> stance. That said, the Committee was more split this time, voting 4-2 in favor of hold - in comparison to 5-1 in the past. Two external members voted for a cut. The RBI retained its 2024-25 inflation forecast at 4.5%, but marked up its GDP growth forecast to 7.2%. Other than that, there were few changes in the statement, with the MPC noting again that, “for the final descent of inflation to the target and its anchoring, monetary policy has to be watchful of spillovers from food price pressures to core inflation and inflation expectations. The MPC will remain resolute in its commitment to aligning inflation to the 4 per cent target on a durable basis.<sup>”</sup>

Our call for monetary easing this fiscal year is predicated on a normal monsoon and a slowing of growth momentum. Recall, even as GDP growth has surprised to the upside, GVA growth – better representative of underlying business cycle dynamics – has slowed from 8.3% to 6.3% over the last four quarters. Therefore, if growth undershoots the RBI<sup>’</sup>s expectations, we believe space for easing will open up.

That said, today<sup>’</sup>s hold was always expected because of the multiple uncertainties the RBI will confront between now and its August review: the progress of the monsoon, the first Budget of the new administration, and two Fed meetings. The Governor noted that the RBI will not mechanically “<sup>‘</sup>follow the Fed,<sup>”</sup> noting that “while we do consider the impact of monetary policy in advanced economies on Indian markets, our actions are primarily determined by domestic growth-inflation conditions and the outlook.<sup>”</sup>

Given the multiple uncertainties shaping the outlook, we remain more agnostic about the timing of the easing cycle. While we have used the August meeting as a placeholder for the first cut, much will depend on how the aforementioned sources of uncertainty resolve between now and then.

## BJP-led coalition to form government

After India<sup>’</sup>s general elections, the incumbent governing coalition – the BJP-led National Democratic Alliance (NDA) – is poised to form a government again, albeit with a reduced majority. The NDA has secured 293 parliamentary seats, above the halfway mark of 272, in a parliament of 543 seats. The main pole of the NDA, the BJP, has slipped below the

halfway mark, securing 240 seats. Unlike the last two terms, therefore, the BJP will be reliant on its allies to form a coalition government. The opposition alliance (INDIA) has secured 232 seats, surpassing exit poll predictions. Government formation is expected to gain momentum in the coming days, with the Prime Minister expected to be sworn in on Sunday, 9 June.

## Asia Focus: Tourism monitor

The number of foreign tourists arriving in Asia steadily rose until 1Q24, then took a breather in April (Figure 1). By origin, Chinese tourists started to show a post-pandemic rebound about a year later than others, faltered in 4Q23, and then reaccelerated sharply early this year. This compensated for the recent slowdown in non-Chinese tourist inflows, which had surpassed pre-pandemic levels earlier this year.

Figure 1: Number of tourist arrivals in Asia by origin

%, compared with same period of 2017-19

![](images/4ebdaf26dbe2acea14b582df007f5a984823b82c8eb6fdcaf9c3183b5de567ba.jpg)

By destination, Vietnam and Korea have seen the largest gains in the last three months (Figure 2), likely benefiting from the renewed increase in Chinese tourist arrivals, upon which they heavily depended since before the pandemic (Figure 3). Japan also stands out for exceeding pre-pandemic levels, not only in visitor numbers but also in tourism revenue (BOP basis, Figure 4), which could be attributed to the enhanced price competitiveness resulting from JPY<sup>’</sup>s depreciation.

%, compared with same period of 2017-19, 3m avg

![](images/843b64a77287a3a6944b3c3ecd3d33a194b11fcd57dcb3383baf4b94f04e2784.jpg)
Source: National tourism offices, J.P. Morgan

Figure 2: Tourist arrivals in Asia by destination
Figure 3: Pre-pandemic mix of tourism source markets (2019)
![](images/8754e8585539e243ff380bb3948962e4cd6fe0bd815498590d8eb6dbb53fe0aa.jpg)

Figure 4: Travel services exports in BOP
![](images/f9439dc45ac82320e33cc4307f115e49028d39a9ad1fd68ee10035d1a46f3dbc.jpg)
Source: IMF, National central banks, J.P. Morgan. \* 2H23 is the latest for PH and TH.

Interestingly, the Philippines has seen a slower recovery in tourist numbers than peers but already outperformed pre-pandemic levels in tourism revenue in 2H23. This might be due to the greater proportion of high-spending Americans and Koreans in its tourist mix, who made up 18% and 29% of visitors in 2023 (Figure 5)—significantly above the regional average of 6% and 14%, and up by 4%-pt and 5%-pt from 2019, respectively.

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer<sup>’</sup>s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer<sup>’</sup>s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its debt obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
